Christina Applegate Thinks Breast Implants Are Making Her Sick

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

After Christina Applegate shared she was misdiagnosed with multiple sclerosis, she questioned if her breast implants could be the cause of her health issues.

Christina Applegate is still on a quest for answers.

Weeks after the Married…With Children alum shared that she had been misdiagnosed with multiple sclerosis, she began questioning if the breast implants she received after her 2008 breast cancer diagnosis could be causing her health issues.

“I’ve been thinking this for years,” Applegate shared with her MeSsy podcast host Jamie-Lynn Sigler on the Nov. 10 episode. “Is the breast implant poisoning? Because I actually had my double mastectomy in 2008 and I am way overdue for having them replaced.”

The 54-year-old added, “I swear, over the last five years or whatever, I thought, ‘What if it's seriously, what if they're leaking and they're poisoning me?’”

Her thoughts on the matter were also amplified after reading a study about breast implant poisoning “presenting as MS.”

“So that's one that I will be looking into,” Applegate noted, “because it's something I've thought about.”

The Dead to Me actress—who shares 15-year-old daughter Sadie with husband Martyn LeNoble—has been candid about her health struggles since she first shared her MS diagnosis in 2021. However, now that she’s learned she was misdiagnosed, she’s struggling to find out what is actually causing her issues.

"This may come as great news,” she said on the Oct. 20 episode of MeSsy, “but to me it comes as, like, I'm back at square one.”

That’s why when her doctor "came into my room and said, 'Congratulations, you don't have MS,’” Applegate recalled, "I was, like, not happy at all. Everybody else was so happy."

She also felt particularly discouraged as she explained to Sigler that she hasn’t “gotten better,” adding,  "If anything, I've gotten worse. So I'm back at square one where I don't know what's going on with me."

And she knew it would be hard for doctors to pinpoint a new diagnosis as she has “every symptom of someone with MS,” noting that there are many illnesses that can mirror MS.

Still, despite not having the disease, she shared that she was grateful to the MS community and continues to feel a part of it, despite her misdiagnosis.

“You guys are my people,” Applegate said on MeSsy Oct. 27. “Even though I don’t have this disease, I have fallen in love with everyone that we know—listeners and our friends—that have MS and I’m still your warrior.”

“I’m still going to speak out on your behalf for ever and ever,” she assured. “I still feel like part of this community. No matter what, I love you.”

For a look at Applegate’s health journey over the years, keep reading.

Christina Applegate shared she was diagnosed with multiple sclerosis in 2021.

"Hi friends. A few months ago I was diagnosed with MS," she shared on her social media channels that August. "It's been a strange journey. But I have been so supported by people that I know who also have this condition. It's been a tough road. But as we all know, the road keeps going. Unless some a--hole blocks it."

"Oh, by the way, I have a disease," she joked during her November 2022 Hollywood Walk of Fame induction ceremony, her first public appearance since her MS diagnosis. "Did you not notice? I'm not even wearing shoes."

Applegate believes her MS journey actually began "six or seven years" before her 2021 diagnosis.

"I noticed, especially the first season [of Dead to Me], we'd be shooting and my leg would buckle," Applegate explained during a March 2024 interview on Good Morning America. "I really just put it off as being tired, or I'm dehydrated, or it's the weather. Then nothing would happen for months, and I didn't pay attention."

By the time she was shooting the Netflix series' third and final season, the actress said she was "being brought to set in a wheelchair."

"I couldn't move that far," she recalled, "so I had to tell everybody because I needed help."

The Dead to Me star captioned this photo of her cane collection amid her battle with MS: "Walking sticks are now part of my new normal."

Joined by her daughter Sadie Grace LeNoble, Applegate had a simple message for multiple sclerosis while attending the 2023 SAG Awards: "F U MS."

And she's not the only one experiencing strong feelings towards the illness. "In my situation, Sadie only knew me as healthy and a runner and a Pelotoner and a dancer—and she only knew that," Applegate explained to friend Jamie-Lynn Sigler on an August 2025 episode of their MeSsy podcast. "So then when this came about in 2021, she was, like, stoic about it."

Yet, "I see her look at me when I’m in bed," Applegate continued, "and can't quite move or, I want to go say goodnight to her in her room but I can’t quite get down the hallway for whatever reason my legs aren’t working that day."

The whole experience has "broken" her teen, Applegate noted. "It was like losing the mom she had to this f--king thing. And the more she’s gotten older now, I think the more it’s hurting her."

After a receiving standing ovation at the 2023 Emmys, the Married...With Children alum quipped, "You're totally shaming me with disability by standing up."

"I make these jokes because if I don't, I'll suffocate," Applegate shared on a March 2024 episode on Armchair Expert, explaining why she often pokes fun at her condition. "I'll be done."

So, yes, she's going to talk about the fact that she's already purchased burial plots.

"My friend and I are going to go take a picnic there," Applegate revealed on a March 2026 episode of NPR's Wild Card. "It's really pretty where it is." She just has to pick out a tree "'cause it's really sunny," she continued. "And I want my visitors to not be sweating."

"I have 30 lesions on my brain," she noted on Armchair Expert. "My biggest one is behind my right eye, so my right eye hurts a lot."

Legions are caused by the immune system attacking the myelin sheath around nerves, according to the Multiple Sclerosis Trust.

Applegate hasn’t held back when it comes to sharing her journey.

"It sucks," the Anchorman actress told late night talk show host Jimmy Kimmel of her daily struggles with MS. "I'm not going to lie. I think anyone who has MS isn't going to be like, 'This is the best thing that ever happened to me!'"

Besides, she'd taken that tack when discussing her breast cancer battle back in 2008. "I went out, and I was the good girl talking about 'Oh, I love my new boobs' that are all scarred and f--ked up. What was I thinking?" she said on Armchair Expert. "Everything I was saying was a freaking lie. It was me trying to convince myself of something, and I think that did no service to anyone."

While she started a foundation and "raised millions of dollars for women to get MRIs who were at high risk," she noted, "at the back of it, I was taking off my bra and crying every night. And I wish that I had said that."

When talking about her day-to-day life with the disease, Applegate told Good Morning America in March 2024, "I live kind of in hell."

"But I might get to a place where I function a little bit better," she added. "Right now, I'm isolating, and that's kind of how I'm dealing with it—by not going anywhere because I don't want to do it. It's hard."

Christina credited her Sweetest Thing costar Selma Blair—who was diagnosed with MS in 2018—for urging her to get tested for the disease.

"She said, 'You need to get checked for MS,'" Christina recalled during her GMA interview. "If not for her, it could've been way worse."

Applegate confirmed that Dead to Me will likely be her last onscreen credit, telling Vanity Fair in May 2023, "I can’t even imagine going to set right now."

"I’m probably not going to work on-camera again, but I'm so glad that I went out with someone who is by far the greatest actress I’ve ever worked with in my entire life," she said of costar Linda Cardellini, "if not the greatest human I’ve ever known.

For Applegate, contracting sapovirus—a virus commonly transmitted through fecal-oral contact—meant having to rely on adult diapers.

"I woke up at three o'clock in the morning in a pool of s--t," she explained on an April 2024 episode of her MeSsy podcast. "I didn't know what happened, and having MS at three o'clock in the morning and trying to change your sheets, it's not fun. But this brings me to my next point, which we can talk about: I'm wearing diapers."

Their discussion was "so liberating," her co-host Sigler noted. "This is something you do by yourself and it feels terrible when you're doing it. I've done that hundreds of times in my MS journey through the years when I've experienced a flair or when I was sick."

"I am in a depression right now, which I don't think I've felt for years," Applegate confessed to Sigler on another 2024 episode of their MeSsy podcast. "Like a real, f--k it all, like real depression where it's kind of scaring me too a little bit because it feels really fatalistic."

Three years into her diagnosis, she continued, "And I'm still sitting here like ‘Boohoo, woe is me.' I'm still mad about it." But the truth was, added, "I don't enjoy living—I don't enjoy things anymore."

Applegate acknowledged the reality of living with MS can be grim. “Everybody has different ways of it showing up,” she noted on a November 2024 episode of her podcast. "I lay in bed screaming. Like, the sharp pains, the ache, the squeezing."

At times, the discomfort even makes it hard for her to enjoy her one source of comfort, the reality TV that at times is "on 24/7 in my room," she noted. But, "I can’t even pick up my phone sometimes," Applegate added, "because now it’s traveled into my hands. So I’ll try to go get my phone or get my remote to turn on the TV or whatever, and sometimes I can’t even hold them."

Since her 2021 diagnosis, Applegate has gotten used to the feel of an open-front gown. "I've been in the hospital upwards of 30 times from throwing up and diarrhea and pain," she relayed on her pocast in March 2025. "That is unimaginable, OK? They’ve done every test known to man on me, put so much radiation into my body from CT scans to everything else."

In the plus column, she noted, she has connected some of her gastro issues to her larger illness. "I have noticed that—and I’m going to be really honest—if I have to poop, I puke," she detailed. "And when I puke, I get all the pain and then all the things happen."

Mere months after her hospital confession, Applegate was checking back in. Returning from a family trip to Europe, "I'm like, 'I wanna be admitted. I'm staying here because I want answers,'" she recounted that August of what she told staff. "'I want every test that you can possibly think of or ones that you haven't even thought of, and I want them done.'"

Following a middle-of-the-night CT, she learned, "I had kidney infections."

More than five years after sharing her battle with multiple sclerosis, she learned that she was misdiagnosed.

"She came into my room and said, 'Congratulations, you don't have MS,'" she recalled of her doctor's visit in the October 2026 podcast MeSsy. "I was, like, not happy at all. Everybody else was so happy."

Applegate emphasized that the confirmation of the misdiagnosis hadn't changed how she's continued to struggle physically.

"I haven't gotten better," she said. "If anything, I've gotten worse. So I'm back at square one where I don't know what's going on with me. That's going to be an online investigation that my doctors are going to be getting into just to find out what has been going on since 2021."

For Keeps: 6 Timeless Jewelry Pieces on One Vogue Editor’s Wish List

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Presented by Chanel Fine Jewelry and Watches.

My jewelry box is as intimate and personal as my handbag. It holds memories, receipts (in value, not literally), collected treasures from vacations, and occasionally even keys—these are for lockets, not doors. But while my daily handbag may change, my jewelry is a permanent fixture: worn on repeat, every piece chosen for keeps.

There are aesthetic signatures I look for in jewelry: delicate diamonds (a sprinkle being my favorite), a sculptural shape, and a strong gold glint. These are the qualities that represent my personal style and punctuate my everyday uniform; they’re finishing touches I need to feel “ready” before leaving the house.

There’s little more satisfying than playing and layering with my jewelry in the morning, sometimes forgetting pieces, other times over-stacking them. It’s this spontaneous approach that inspired me to take stock of my collection: What’s missing, what’s staying, and what, wistfully, might be time to part with. Consider this my attempt at building the ultimate jewelry box capsule, anchored by six Chanel fine jewelry and watches icons.

I already mentioned I’m an over-stacker, but I love how the number of rings I wear often reflects how I’m feeling. On Mondays, I might leave the house wearing just one simple gold band—to feel low-key, and ease myself into the week. On a bold day, I love to layer and the Coco Crush family is perfectly fit for my stacking needs. Four is my magic number, to accommodate various sizes and finishes.

Chanel’s Première collection has been on my wish list for a long time—10 years to be precise from when I first saw the Originale at a Chanel boutique in Harrods. (I still have the photo of my wrist from that first try-on.) Its bracelet-like quality is elegant yet functional, though a decade on, I’m equally enamored by the Première Galon and its braided motif. If I had to choose just one, the Originale would be the way to go, purely for the sentimental reasons.

While I champion simplicity in how I dress, it’s rare that I put on a watch and don’t add a companion next to it—either a diamond bracelet, a vintage chain, or a simple bangle. This elegant gold bangle is effortless and will quite literally go with any watch you already own.

A jewelry box curation isn’t complete without a gold chain necklace, I don’t believe anyone who enjoys jewelry can go without one. From dainty delicate chains, to chunkier T-bar clasps and even those that come with a pendant or medallion, the options are endless. I’m settling for something simple, to be styled over a white shirt, or layered over a turtleneck for high contrast. I always want the gold to pop.

A watch should be timeless (excuse the pun), but that doesn't mean it can't feel current. Today, the cocktail watch is trending. Traditionally a very small, jewelry-like watch worn for evening, the timepiece has taken on a new, everyday end-use that can elevate the simplest of outfits. Layer over the sleeves black turtleneck, styled alongside a silk scarf or paired with a diamanté bracelet—the styling opportunities are endless. I especially love designs with contrast like the Chanel Première Ribbon; it feels modern with its sporty-like rubber strap but the black lacquer face and diamond trim give it an ornate quality, reminiscent of the designs of yore.

I have eight ear piercings and counting and love nothing more than a simple but statement-making gold hoop. The quilted motif on the Coco Crush earrings have just the right amount of texture—I’d love to stack them up the side of my ear but that feels ambitious. Once they’re in, I find it hard to imagine wearing any other style.

Our Universe Is Held Together by Invisible Galactic Glue—And We Wouldn’t Exist Without It

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Its transformative emergence might have occurred within the very first seconds of time, physicists say.

Dark matter isn’t like other matter. It doesn’t interact with visible light or any other electromagnetic force. And yet, it seems to push around regular matter through its gravitational influence. Dark matter rules the universe, maybe holding galaxies together, but we still don’t know what it is.

A battery of cosmological probes has revealed clues to the presence—and influence—of dark matter in the universe: Stars orbit galactic centers far too quickly. Galaxies zip around clusters with excessive velocities. Massive objects bend the light passing by them with more gravitational force than we can account for with visible observations. And the history of the universe has revealed the incredibly speedy growth of gigantic structures.

The conclusion is that there’s something more to the universe than our traditional electromagnetic measurements can detect. Yet this thing simply doesn’t emit or absorb light. What could it be?

What we know is that it’s cold, heavy, and slow. Yet, new research suggests that it may have once been the complete opposite: hot, light, fast … and insignificant.

How do you turn dark matter from zero to hero? The answer lies in the strange and wonderful physics of superconductivity, which lets electricity flow with zero resistance. In a new paper, theoretical physicists Guanming Liang and Robert Caldwell from Dartmouth College outline a hypothetical scenario for flooding the universe with dark matter when it was mere seconds old.

For decades, cosmologists have been following one particular compelling idea to explain dark matter, known as WIMPs, for weakly interacting massive particles. These are hypothetical particles that only interact with the rest of the universe through gravity and the weak nuclear force (which rules subatomic particles’ behavior). But searches for WIMPs have so far come up empty.

To fill the gaps, theorists have been busy, generating dozens of new theories every year to unravel the mystery of dark matter. The vast majority of these ideas come from the land of high-energy physics, invoking this-or-that exotic process or super-theoretical particle that we might see someday in a collider. These massive machines smash high-energy subatomic particles together in the search for new particles.

But Liang and Caldwell took a different tack, turning instead toward condensed matter physics. This branch of physics makes major breakthroughs all the time, but without as much public fanfare as the high-energy work.

At a fundamental level, condensed matter physics is the study of stuff. It gives us an understanding of how everyday things work on subatomic levels—from why glass is transparent to how heat flows through new materials. And it’s also the study of everyday materials under extreme conditions, which leads to completely counterintuitive behaviors.

For example, take electrical resistance. Electrons flowing down a wire bump into each other, which saps energy out. This is why we can’t, for instance, have solar arrays in the desert that can pipe their electricity all around the country.

But if you cool some materials down to nearly absolute zero, the electrons bind up together in a new state of matter. And when they do that, all the resistance disappears, and electricity can flow uninterrupted. This is known as superconductivity, and it’s through superconductors that we’re able to harness tremendously large currents to drive technologies like MRI machines and maglev (magnetic levitation) trains.

In Liang and Caldwell’s model, dark matter starts out as a hot soup of fast-moving, massless particles in the extremely early universe. But as the universe expands and cools off, the dark matter particles pair together, the way electrons do in a superconductor. When they do that, the dark matter particles undergo a radical transformation, emerging on the other side with a brand-new set of properties. The pairs become slow-moving, heavy—and dark.

However, the model revealed an unusual quirk: that if the dark matter particles start out with even a tiny bit of mass, then not all of them bind up together as pairs. Most of them stay in the background, where they soak up all of spacetime. This behavior might provide an explanation for another mysterious ingredient in the cosmos, dark energy, which is responsible for causing the accelerated expansion of the universe.

So far Liang and Caldwell’s idea is just that: a guess as to how the universe might actually work. But it’s a testable one. The properties of these paired-up dark matter particles may leave behind a subtle signature in the appearance of the cosmic microwave background. That’s the light left over from when the universe cooled from a plasma to a neutral state, when it was around 380,000 years old.

To be fair, the idea is probably wrong. But that’s okay. We have no idea what dark matter is, and we need to flex every analytical and creative muscle we have to solve a puzzle that spans the entire universe. And we need to draw from as many sources of inspiration as possible, whether it’s high-energy physics, condensed matter physics—or even something else.

We never know when we’re going to be right. It’s only by searching in the dark that we’ll find out.

Paul M. Sutter is a science educator and a cosmologist at Johns Hopkins University and the author of How to Die in Space: A Journey Through Dangerous Astrophysical Phenomena and Your Place in the Universe: Understanding Our Big, Messy Existence. Sutter is also the host of various science programs, and he’s on social media. Check out his Ask a Spaceman podcast and his YouTube page.

Peruvian senator calls for removal of large-scale replicas of Moche erotic sculptures from public park

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

One of the huaco erótico sculptures in Fertility Park, near Trujillo, Peru Photo: Richard Avila, via Wikimedia Commons

A controversy over 3m-tall erotic sculptures in northern Peru reignited recently after a senator requested that the cultural ministry remove them from a public area. Located in a park outside the city of Trujillo, the sculptures are replicas of ceramics from the pre-Incan Moche civilisation.

“What happens when children see these images? Neurological and psychological factors are affected, because they see body dimensions that are completely different from how they see themselves,” Milagros Jáuregui, a conservative senator from the Renovación Popular party, said in a speech on 26 October.

The sculptures are located in Parque de la Fertilidad (Fertility Park), a public space where in 2021 then-mayor Arturo Fernández of the Moche district began installing giant fibreglass reproductions of ceramic depictions of sexual acts to promote tourism.

Known as huacos eróticos, the much smaller original ceramics date from the 3rd to 8th century AD. No more than 30cm in height, they were placed in tombs as burial offerings. But the debate about their much larger versions in the park goes beyond political prudishness.

“It’s a complicated issue,” the Peruvian archaeologist Walter Alva tells The Art Newspaper. “There’s freedom to express or re-create themes from the past, but these ceramics were created to be placed as offerings to the dead, with a sense of revitalising life. They are associated with both fertility and an entire system of beliefs.”

An original huaco erótico ceramic offering made by the Moche civilisation, Larco Museum, Lima Photo: László Varga, via Wikimedia Commons

The Moche civilisation covered 700km of the coast of what is now Peru, between the regions of Piura and Ancash, centuries before the Inca dominated the area. “Moche art is primarily ceramic, and these vessels depict erotic scenes or concepts of sexuality from that era,” Alva says. “The sculptures in the park were created outside of their historical context.”

One of the greatest archaeological discoveries from the Moche civilisation was that of the Lord of Sipán near the city of Chiclayo—a discovery Alva made in 1987. A 3rd-century monarch, the Lord of Sipán’s body was found covered with gold along with eight other people, a dog and two llamas and nearly 500 objects including ceramic vessels, ornaments and textiles.

The Moche have the largest number of erotic representations among pre-Hispanic civilisations. The most complete collection of its ceramics is in Lima, at the Larco Museum of pre-Columbian art, which celebrated its centennial in July. Before the 1960s, children were forbidden from entering the room that housed the huacos. Today, minors must be accompanied by an adult. The institution’s Erotic Gallery is one of its most popular attractions.

Jáuregui, an evangelical pastor as well as a senator, believes these Moche sculptures should only be in closed spaces. “Displaying these types of images in public is harmful to children,” she told congress. “We should have them in museums, where high-school students can observe a part of our culture that is a national treasure with the help of teachers or some kind of tutor.”

Excavations at the Cerro Mayal site in the north of the country show it was an important production facility for Moche ceramics around the seventh century

Many of the antiquities being returned were looted from northern Peru in the 1960s and 70s

The so-called “Hall of the Moche Imaginary” is one of two elaborately decorated spaces archaeologists recently uncovered at Pañamarca

Why Prince William Is Reluctant to Become Head of the Commonwealth

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Following King Charles’s successful nine-day tour to the Caribbean, the future of the Commonwealth has become a much debated subject. At last week’s Commonwealth Heads of Government Meeting (CHOGM) in Antigua and Barbuda, Charles, who was designated Head of the Commonwealth, a role he assumed after his late mother Queen Elizabeth, told assembled leaders he found the voluntary union of 56 nations to be a “source of enormous encouragement and pride.”

However, Buckingham Palace has made it clear this weekend that Charles will only serve as monarch in the Caribbean realms “for as long as they wish” and that the monarch is open to change. According to The Times, Charles is “fully open” to those realms removing him as head of state.

While Charles was in the Caribbean, there was ongoing debate about slave trade reparations, which the monarch addressed in his speech to CHOGM. Describing it as a “monstrous injustice” he expressed his “profound personal sorrow” but fell short of making a formal apology for Britain’s role in the slave trade. (Since King Charles is a ceremonial head of state, he is unable to apologize on behalf of Britain unless the government sanctions it.)

Amid calls for reparations, there are questions about the future of the Commonwealth and who its next head should be. In conversation with Vanity Fair, constitutional expert Ed Owens, author of After Elizabeth: Can the Monarchy Save Itself?, discusses what’s in store for the future.

This interview has been edited and condensed for clarity.

King Charles reacts near Antigua and Barbuda flags during a visit to Nelson's Dockyard, a historic naval base established in 1725, during his visit to Antigua and Barbuda on November 2, 2026 in St John's, Antigua and Barbuda.

Vanity Fair: Prince William has made it clear that he does not necessarily expect to follow his father as head of the Commonwealth, saying that the decision belongs to the Commonwealth itself.

Ed Owens: Prince William hinted after his difficult 2022 tour of Belize, the Bahamas, and Jamaica that he wasn’t focused on following his father as head of the Commonwealth. A couple of weeks ago The Times of London confirmed that the prince’s position was unchanged on this issue. Crucially, the position of head of the Commonwealth is not hereditary. King Charles got the role because his mother and predecessor, Queen Elizabeth II, actively lobbied Commonwealth heads of state to ensure that the role would pass to him when he became monarch, following her death. But it will not automatically transfer to William when Charles dies.

What is the Commonwealth and how significant is that statement?

The Commonwealth of Nations (as it’s officially known) is a voluntary association of 56 independent member states. Most of these member states were once colonies in the British empire. Decolonization began after World War II and most newly independent states—though no longer part of the empire—became part of the new Commonwealth. This new international body represented change but also continuity, enabling Britain to maintain a global (but mostly symbolic) presence at a time when its real geopolitical power was rapidly in retreat. The Commonwealth also provided a stage for the monarchy to continue to exercise an important international role. George VI was the last emperor and first Head of the Commonwealth. The latter role was invented by the British government to replace the former and allowed the monarch to maintain a semblance of global prestige while helping to facilitate the process of decolonization.

Although most member states have historical ties to Britain, any country can apply to join the Commonwealth—exemplified by recent members like Rwanda, Mozambique, Gabon, and Togo. Members commit to shared principles outlined in the Commonwealth charter, including democracy, human rights, the rule of law, and sustainable development. In reality, though, many member states have failed to live up to these principles, and the Commonwealth has, in recent years, felt morally directionless.

William’s reluctance to take on the mantle of Head of the Commonwealth isn’t that surprising: He and his advisers want to future-proof his reign. One way to do this is to identify potential problems in order to avoid them. The Commonwealth is one such potential problem, and William seems to have calculated that the headship of this organization likely represents more trouble than it is worth.

Does it represent a fundamental shift in the monarchy’s attitude toward the organization, or simply an acknowledgement that the old imperial relationship is no longer sustainable?

We have to remember that 15 members of the Commonwealth (including the UK) are also “realms”—i.e. their head of state is King Charles. I think it’s clear even to the current king that his position as monarch of the 14 realms beyond Britain is unsustainable. Eventually all 14 nations, and perhaps the UK too, will opt to become republics, cutting their ties with the crown. Those nations most likely to become republics soonest—owing to the fact that they have surging republican movements—are the Caribbean realms, two of which (the Bahamas and Antigua and Barbuda) King Charles visited during his recent tour to the Caribbean. Indeed, we can even interpret the tour as the king and his courtiers preparing the ground for this transition to republics. This is because, though former realms will no longer have as their head of state the incumbent monarch, they will almost certainly remain members of the wider Commonwealth.

What King Charles was trying to do with his latest tour was to inject energy and meaning into the idea of the Commonwealth, more than attempt to reinvigorate the personal bonds that connect him to his peoples in the Caribbean. In loudly acknowledging the injustices of the slave trade in which Britain was, for a long time, complicit, while also reminding his hosts that Britain was one of the first major powers to abolish and oppose slavery—the king has sought to address and ease concerns in ways that might enable the Commonwealth to function more cohesively in the future, irrespective of whether the UK’s monarch remains in post as head of the Commonwealth.

When Charles eventually leaves the throne, could the Commonwealth decide that it no longer needs a British monarch as its figurehead? What might replace that role? Will the Commonwealth continue?

I absolutely think [the] Commonwealth nations will look to replace the British monarch with a new figurehead. My sense is that many nations would already prefer to have a different kind of figurehead, for example a head who belongs to one of the 56 member states and is elected by member states for a set term. Alternatively, there could be some kind of “rolling” headship, whereby every member nation is invited to elect an official who will take a 12-month turn as head of the Commonwealth.

Whether or not the Commonwealth will continue is more difficult to say. Certainly one of the reasons it has survived until now is because of the energy and engagement of two British monarchs—Charles and Elizabeth II—who have viewed it as a potential force for good in the world. Without its connection to the British monarchy, it may lose what is left of its momentum. However, its survival also depends on wider geopolitical shifts: If individual governments within the Commonwealth view it as [a] useful arena for bringing together states who could benefit from closer association—be that economic, political, or military association—then it may continue long into the future.

There is an increasingly difficult conversation about Britain’s colonial past, slavery, and reparations. Charles has expressed personal sorrow over the slave trade, but the question of a formal apology remains politically and constitutionally complicated. How much freedom does the king actually have to address these issues, and could William take a different approach?

The king has very little freedom to apologize unless his government specifically sanctions and encourages such an apology. As the recent Caribbean tour made clear, I think Charles would prefer to strike a more nuanced line, reminding audiences that, despite the UK’s colonial past and its involvement in the horrors of slavery, Britain was one of the first major powers to abolish slavery and that it played an important role in bringing to an end the maritime trade in human chattel. Whether William takes a different approach will depend entirely on the UK government’s approach to formal apologies and reparations. My sense is that no government—at least not now or in the near-to-medium-term future—will agree to financial reparations, because historians, philosophers, and politicians are increasingly pushing back on such a solution, questioning why UK taxpayers (already under huge strain) should fork out huge sums for the wrongs committed by some of their ancestors more than two centuries ago.

What would Queen Elizabeth II make of this? She saw the Commonwealth as her father’s legacy and her second family.

I think she would probably be relatively relaxed, recognizing that times change and institutions have to evolve. Let’s not forget that as queen she oversaw many key alterations in the way both the monarchy and Commonwealth functioned through her long reign, as was required by shifts in politics, economics, culture, and social norms. While the Commonwealth was of emotional importance to her—not least for the way it connected her back to her father and predecessors—by the end of her reign it was already a shadow of what it once had been and she would have known that it was likely to continue on a trajectory of decline. William has decided that given the challenges and changes of the mid-20th century the monarchy and royal family’s future is likely to be stronger without the post-imperial baggage of the Commonwealth. I think his grandmother would have respected the way he is putting the family firm first in this instance.

Wolfsburg’s Muhammed Damar eager to make the most of Germany U21 recall

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Muhammed Damar is back in the German U21 national team after almost a year away. The 22-year-old attacking midfielder’s return comes at an important juncture in a career that has experienced both significant promise and frustrating setbacks over the past two seasons.

Speaking at Tuesday’s DFB U21 press conference, attended remotely by Get German Football News, Damar expressed his delight at rejoining Antonio Di Salvo’s squad ahead of the upcoming internationals against Belgium and Croatia. Damar spoke warmly of the welcome he received from his teammates and the continued support of Di Salvo throughout his absence.

The midfielder’s resurgence at VfL Wolfsburg has provided the foundation for his international comeback. Four goals and one assist across his last six league appearances have helped restore the attacking midfielder’s reputation following an extraordinarily difficult year at Hoffenheim.

Damar’s career has already taken several turns. The Berlin native joined Hoffenheim from Eintracht Frankfurt’s youth setup in 2022. After an unsuccessful loan spell at Hannover 96, he truly announced himself during the 2024/25 campaign at SV Elversberg.

Damar scored nine goals in 31 league appearances for the Saarland club, helping Elversberg reach the Bundesliga promotion play-off. His performances attracted considerable attention from top-flight clubs, including Borussia Dortmund and Stuttgart.

Hoffenheim responded by extending his contract until 2029, with Damar viewed as a potential successor to Bayern Munich-bound midfielder Tom Bischof. The anticipated breakthrough in Sinsheim never materialized.

As GGFN examined during the most recent summer transfer window, Damar struggled to establish himself amid fierce competition in Hoffenheim’s attacking ranks. Andrej Kramaric and Bazoumana Touré stood in his way, leaving the youngster with only two starts across 15 Bundesliga appearances last season.

For a player whose development had accelerated so impressively at Elversberg, the lack of regular football represented a considerable setback.

Damar eventually opted for another opportunity in the 2. Bundesliga. Relegated Wolfsburg secured his services in July, signing the attacking midfielder to a contract through 2031. The decision has already begun paying dividends.

The 22-year-old has started ten of Wolfsburg’s first eleven league matches under head coach Tobias Strobl. Primarily operating in an advanced midfield role, Damar has also featured centrally when required. More importantly, the regular minutes have allowed him to rediscover the attacking qualities that made him such an intriguing prospect at Elversberg.

It wasn’t an immediate turnaround. Damar failed to register a goal or assist in Wolfsburg’s opening five league fixtures, but his fortunes began changing in September. The first breakthrough came in Wolfsburg’s emphatic 5-1 victory over Darmstadt on September 18. Damar converted a Joakim Mæhle assist in the 67th minute to put his team 4-1 ahead. The goal marked the beginning of a productive run that continued into October.

In Wolfsburg’s extraordinary 4-4 draw against Nürnberg on October 10, Damar doubled his team’s advantage with a well-placed long-range effort in the 29th minute. He attempted five shots during the match, placing three on target.

One week later, Damar again made a decisive contribution in Wolfsburg’s 3-1 victory over fellow Bundesliga relegation sufferers Heidenheim. With his team defending a narrow 2-1 lead, Damar latched onto a Fabian Reese layoff outside the penalty area and unleashed an effort that deflected into the net in the 65th minute.

The midfielder was particularly active in the encounter, recording six shots, four of which were on target. He also covered 12 kilometres, more than any other Wolfsburg player, and subsequently received the club’s Player of the Match award following a supporters’ vote.

Damar’s recent performances have not been limited to goalscoring. In Wolfsburg’s 1-1 draw at Bochum on October 23, he registered the assist for Reese’s opening goal, switching play from the right half-space to the left side of the penalty area. Reese finished with an impressive volley.

Five days later, Damar supplied another assist in the DFB-Pokal against SC Freiburg. His sharply delivered 43rd-minute corner found Kento Shiogai at the near post, allowing the Japanese international to head Wolfsburg into a 2-1 lead. The performance earned Damar a rating of six in GGFN‘s firsthand match assessment, although Freiburg eventually prevailed 3-2 following two late Keisuke Goto goals.

Damar returned to the scoresheet in Wolfsburg’s subsequent 2-0 league victory over Osnabrück. Once again combining effectively with Reese, the attacking midfielder met his teammate’s cutback and guided a technically accomplished right-footed finish into the bottom corner in the 50th minute.

Damar’s four league goals and one assist across his last six appearances, supplemented by a cup assist, reflect a player who is once again influencing matches through shooting, combination play, and set-piece delivery.

Crucially, he is doing so while receiving the consistent opportunities that proved so elusive at Hoffenheim.

Interestingly, Damar revealed at Tuesday’s press conference that a return to Elversberg had also been discussed before his Wolfsburg move. Responding to a question from Kicker’s Michael Pfeiffer, the midfielder confirmed that he remained in contact with current Elversberg coach Vincent Wagner, whom he knew from their time together at Hoffenheim.

“I know Vincent Wagner from Hoffenheim’s U23,” Damar explained. “Whenever I wasn’t playing with the first team, I went down to the U23s. We’ve maintained a very good relationship. We exchanged messages, but in the end I decided on Wolfsburg.”

He nevertheless retained considerable affection for his former loan club.

“Elversberg is an excellent place for young players,” Damar said. “You receive enormous trust from everyone there and can play football freely, without pressure.”

His assessment was echoed by fellow press conference attendee Maurice Krattenmacher, who joined Elversberg from Bayern Munich in the summer of 2026 and earned a late call-up to the U21s ahead of the current break. The 21-year-old highlighted the club’s willingness to invest in youngsters and provide opportunities even when their initial performances fall short of expectations.

“As a young player, you get your chance there,” Krattenmacher explained at the press conference. “And even if you don’t quite make the most of that chance, you get a second one. The team also welcomes you very well. I think it’s the combination of trust and good coaches that makes the difference.”

There is another important dimension to Damar’s return. His previous international performances had provided compelling evidence of his potential even while his club career remained frustratingly stagnant. GGFN repeatedly highlighted the youngster during Germany’s 2025 U21 European Championship qualifying campaign.

Our September tactical assessment praised Damar’s strong second-half performance against Albania and his goal in the subsequent 5-0 victory over Latvia. The midfielder again featured among our six standout performers from the October international break.

Despite Germany’s 3-2 defeat against Greece, Damar impressed with his work rate, attacking involvement, and goal. His contribution against Northern Ireland offered further evidence of his value to Di Salvo’s squad.

By the end of the November 2025 internationals, Damar had scored three goals in six U21 appearances. GGFN‘s November tactical report again identified him as a standout performer.

MARA dumps Bitcoin to erase a $750M collateral risk

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

MARA repaid its Bitcoin-backed loans, but 2027 bond puts, cash resources and spending choices shape the case for further sales.

Bitcoin miner MARA Holdings has repaid its $750 million of Bitcoin-backed loans, ending the collateral-liquidation exposure attached to those facilities.

Further coin sales could now serve to build cash for bondholder demands, AI infrastructure, and possible share buybacks.

The company's Nov. 9 filing disclosed that it sold 13,597 Bitcoin after Sept. 30 for approximately $1.1 billion and used $750 million to repay the loans in full.

Management's quarterly letter says it intends to use Bitcoin to build cash for AI infrastructure opportunities, convertible-note puts exercisable next year and potential repurchases of its own shares.

As of Sept. 30, MARA had $291.6 million of June 2031 notes carrying a June 4, 2027 cash-put right, and $632.5 million of March 2030 notes carrying a December 1, 2027 right. Together, those September balances total about $924.1 million.

A cash put lets a bondholder require the company to buy back all or part of the notes for cash at face value, plus any applicable unpaid special interest.

The notes' contractual maturity dates remain in 2030 and 2031, and the eventual cash bill depends on which holders exercise and how much principal remains outstanding.

A separate obligation of $48 million of notes outstanding at Sept. 30 matures on Dec. 1. The put calendar does not require MARA to sell Bitcoin to meet potential cash demands.

The 2027 holder-put calendar survives the repayment of MARA's Bitcoin-backed loans. Explore how the potential route to selling changed.

The outstanding Coinbase and Two Prime loans exposed pledged Bitcoin to conditional lender liquidation remedies.

The Nov. 9 disclosures report full repayment after quarter-end. Management still identifies cash uses for continued monetization.

Cash puts are elective holder rights. Repayment removes one liquidation channel while management still plans Bitcoin monetization.

MARA entered the fourth quarter with $946.5 million of cash and equivalents, excluding $12 million of restricted cash. Approximately 11% of that cash was held in euros by its majority-owned subsidiary Exaion and contractually designated for its operations over the next year.

The subsequent Bitcoin sales brought in approximately $350 million more than the loan payoff. Adding that difference to September cash produces a rough $1.3 billion bridge before other cash movements.

MARA also reported about $1.5 billion of unused capacity under its at-the-market share issuance program as of Sept. 30. Management said existing liquidity resources provided flexibility to meet obligations and fund growth over the next twelve months and beyond.

MARA used $642.7 million of cash in operating activities during the first nine months of 2026. Its Bitcoin-sale proceeds are classified as investing cash flows, so the operating outflow alone cannot establish a funding gap after those sales.

The planned acquisition of power business Long Ridge remained pending in the Nov. 9 disclosures. Its approximately $1.5 billion enterprise value includes up to about $900 million of assumed debt.

A bridge commitment of up to $785 million supports a portion of that debt if needed, subject to customary conditions.

The data-center development agreement with Starwood includes MARA funds approved for pre-development pursuit costs, with $136.3 million approved across sites and about $17.7 million incurred by Sept. 30.

Site contributions receive value before MARA adds incremental equity, while project financing is expected to be non-recourse.

The HIF site-development project's price of up to $600 million is tied to post-closing development milestones, with additional payments possible for specified shortfalls.

The board's Nov. 9 authorization permits up to $500 million of common-share purchases, but requires no particular amount and can be suspended or terminated.

The next meaningful supply signal is MARA's disclosure of further Bitcoin sales and how it uses the proceeds. Updated cash and remaining note balances will help distinguish preparation for holder puts from optional infrastructure and buyback spending.

SpaceX moves Starship Flight 15 booster to launch pad for 'squeeze test' ahead of epic double-catch attempt (photos)

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

The historic flight is scheduled to launch on Friday (Nov. 13).

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

SpaceX is one big step closer to the 15th test flight of its Starship megarocket.

The company announced today (Nov. 10) that it had moved its latest Starship first stage, known as Super Heavy Booster 22, to the launch mount at its Starbase facility in South Texas ahead of Flight 15, which is scheduled to lift off this Friday (Nov. 13).

During the upcoming launch test, SpaceX plans to catch both Booster 22 and Starship's Ship upper stage with its launch tower's "chopstick" arms in a double landing catch — something the private spaceflight company has never tried before. It will be the first full recovery of an orbital rocket in history.

Booster 22 therefore "was used for final catch testing upon arrival [at the pad], including a suspended squeeze test at catch height," SpaceX said in an X post today (Nov. 10).

That post shared four photos of the giant booster, which is powered by 33 of SpaceX's next-gen Raptor engines. The first shot showed Super Heavy in one of Starbase's "Mega Bay" assembly buildings before rollout, while the other three depicted it grasped by the tower's arms, held high in the South Texas sky.

SpaceX has used the chopstick arms during Starship flights before; it has successfully caught Super Heavy on three separate occasions. But the company has never attempted a catch of Ship, which flies higher and farther than the booster and is therefore a more challenging target. And SpaceX only reached orbit with Starship for the first time on Flight 14, which launched on Sept. 28.

So Flight 15 will break new ground. And, if all goes to plan, it will give us a look at the future: SpaceX plans to catch Super Heavy and Ship on all operational Starship missions, as this recovery strategy will allow the company to boost the megarocket's launch cadence.

And SpaceX wants to fly Starship incredibly often, so that it can build out its Starlink V3 broadband constellation and even bigger Starmind AI data-center network. Indeed, Elon Musk has said the company is eyeing 10,000 Starship flights per year by 2030.

If all goes to plan, Flight 15 will launch from Starbase on Friday during a 75-minute window that opens at 5:45 p.m. EST (2245 GMT; 4:45 p.m. local Texas time).

Super Heavy will return to Starbase for its catch about 6.5 minutes after liftoff. Ship will follow suit much later — about 22 hours later, in fact, after it has deployed 26 Starlink V3 spacecraft into LEO and completed 14 circuits of our planet.

You can watch all the action live here at Space.com and get updates about the mission here.

These Underrated Tools Are Up to 39% Off Ahead of Black Friday

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Shop Black Friday savings on clever, overlooked tools that make everyday projects easier–and prove that the little guys deserve some glory, too.

Gear-obsessed editors choose every product we review. We may earn commission if you buy from a link. Why Trust Us?

Every toolbox has its stars. The cordless drill, the circular saw, the impact driver. Big, powerful tools that get most of the attention. But tucked away in the corners of my own tool drawers are the unsung heroes—those little problem solvers that I barely think of until that moment I desperately need them. And when I do—Wow—I sure am glad they’re there.

I’m talking about the clever tools specifically designed for certain situations that make everyday jobs easier. A Klein Voltage Tester so I don’t mistakenly zap myself as I replace a wall receptacle. A Bosch Laser Measure that saves me from wrestling a tape across a ceiling. Or a Japanese-style pull saw that makes clean, precise cuts the old-school way—by hand. These might not be the flashiest tools in my shop, but I’ve come to dedicate a singular drawer to them as I use them so often.

With Black Friday deals already rolling in, now’s a great time to give these underrated workhorses a little love. I’ve rounded up a few of my favorite useful, affordable unsung-hero-type tools that are currently enjoying some pretty significant discounts. They’re great gifts for the DIYer who seems to have everything, and even better excuses to treat yourself to a tool that perhaps you didn’t know you needed.

Here’s a holiday tool purchase that won’t hurt your gift budget. For less than $20 on sale, this little Klein Voltage Tester deserves a spot in your handyperson’s toolbox. Touch the receiver to a live wire, outlet, or switch and the tester lights up and beeps, indicating a flow of current. I keep mine right in its own small pocket in my tool belt, and another in my tool drawer so it’s an easy grab to make sure that wire isn’t lighting me up in addition to the fixture it’s connected to. Is it a “head-turner” gift? Not really. Is it a gift that will be appreciated for years to come? You bet.

I’m a huge fan of these Craftsman Ratcheting Wrenches, and at 36 percent off, my enthusiasm is growing. There’s nothing like trying to loosen a stubborn bolt in a cramped space, where you barely have room to fit a conventional ratchet, let alone your knuckles. That’s where these wrenches shine. Each has a 90-tooth mechanism requiring only four degrees of swing to engage the next tooth, which makes work in tight quarters possible. This 20-piece set includes both metric and SAE, so you should be covered in terms of sizes across the board. At this price, it’s a lot of wrench for the money.

Ever stretched a tape measure across a wall or ceiling only to have it buckle halfway there? So much for your measurement–and your patience. At just around $50, this Bosch is a smart holiday tool for anyone measuring rooms for flooring, paint, or furniture. It measures distances up to 100 feet and comes with two AA batteries so your lucky DIY enthusiast can put it straight to work.

Nothing ruins your day quite like that little Check Engine light coming on. But before you pay your mechanic just to tell you what’s wrong, plug in this code reader. The ANCEL AD310 reads engine fault codes, gives you a bit of insight into what’s happening beneath the hood, and can clear codes as well. No batteries. No subscriptions. No Bluetooth pairing required. Just more information about what’s going on with your car or truck, which—at $30—is a pretty sweet little tool for a home mechanic, or anyone who wants to know where they stand before taking their vehicle to a shop.

If you’re building a birdhouse, repairing furniture, or taking your first shot at joinery, here’s an inexpensive way to get acquainted with a pull saw if you’ve never owned one before. This $18 saw from RUITOOL is a great entry point for beginning woodworkers–and useful as well for DIYers making small, precision cuts. Its thin blade cuts on the pull-stroke, with separate edges for more difficult cuts across the grain. At more than 20 percent off, it’s an affordable addition to anyone’s woodworking toolbox.

When I first started working at Popular Mechanics, this Klein Hybrid Screwdriver was up for grabs. I practically looked over both shoulders before claiming it. Years of working with tools have taught me to spot a good one, and this one had me at “Free”. It has onboard bit storage, nut drivers, and those bits are impact-rated to boot. Maybe it’s not exactly free, but at $20 for Black Friday, you, too, can have your own little “wait, seriously??” moment.

This is probably the most basic pocket hole jig that Kreg makes. I use mine all the time, mostly because it’s simple to work with. Maybe you’re building a cabinet and you want to hide a fastener in a pre-drilled pocket hole–or have another situation that calls for a hidden screw that won’t crack the material. Simply set the jig stop against the edge of your workpiece, clamp it in place, and use the enclosed drill bit to bore through the guide and into your wood. Perfect pocket holes every time, ready to accept–and hide–your fastener of choice.

These extra-slim pliers are great for reaching nuts and fittings in tight spots. Think plumbing repairs, awkward connections, and spaces where a conventional wrench just won’t cut it. The rubber grips are comfortable on the hands, and I like the serrated teeth in the jaws for gripping and pulling nails (there’s even a handy groove for holding nails and screws in place as you start to drive them). They’re not particularly flashy, but they are a genuinely useful problem-solver. And at nearly 40 percent off, they certainly deserve that spot in my tool drawer.

Have you ever cut a baseboard miter on an outside corner, only to discover when you go to nail it that there’s a sizable gap in the joint? Odds are your miter saw isn’t wrong–the construction of the wall corner is. Sure, there are methods for stacking and scribing lines on the trim to determine the exact angle–or you could simply reach for this Digital Angle Finder by Klein and uncover the truth in seconds. Use this once and you’ll come to realize that caulk isn’t your best friend in these situations–this angle finder is.

Pochettino Would Make Marcus Rashford His Next Son Heung-Min Son at Man Utd

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Manchester United are planning for life after Michael Carrick, and Mauricio Pochettino is the man they are looking to make their next manager.

The Red Devils have won just three of their 14 games in all competitions, including just one of their 10 Premier League games, leaving them 18th in the table heading into the November international break - their worst ever start to a Premier League season.

That form is set to see Carrick sacked, with GIVEMESPORT sources understanding that Sir Jim Ratcliffe will head a meeting in Monaco in Wednesday where his future will be confirmed one way or another. Former Spurs boss Pochettino is considered to be a leading target to replace him, with the USA boss having a $10m (£7.4m) release clause in his contract that would make him available immediately.

During his time in north London, Pochettino rebuilt the squad and saw them reach an EFL Cup and Champions League final, while they also finished second in the Premier League.

A large part of that success was the partnerships he created all over the pitch, with the most notorious being between Heung-Min Son and Harry Kane. The duo have combined for more Premier League goals than any other pairing in competition history, and most of those came under Pochettino's tutelage.

At Chelsea, Pochettino also managed to create a brilliant partnership between Cole Palmer and Nicolas Jackson to help the team qualify for the Champions League before he was rather harshly dismissed after just one campaign at Stamford Bridge.

Now the Argentine will be looking to do similar at Old Trafford, and it's clear that he already has the tools and personnel to at least replicate the South Korean superstar's skillset.

United's attacking options are strong. Bruno Fernandes is the talisman in the number ten role, while Matheus Cunha, Bryan Mbeumo, Amad, Joshua Zirkzee, and Benjamin Sesko provide experience and quality. Shea Lacey provides a wildcard out wide as a youngster, and is someone that Pochettino will love, while Mason Mount can be useful when he's fit.

But the forward who is likely to take the most joy from Pochettino's arrival is Marcus Rashford, and the manager can easily use him as the perfect Heung-Min Son clone in his team.

It was a surprise to see Rashford return to Man Utd in the summer following a successful loan spell at Barcelona, and he started the season well. He created a number of chances for teammates and looked sharp, even without registering a goal or assist.

But the England international hasn't featured since the 2-0 defeat to Atletico Madrid last month due to an ongoing hip issue, missing six games. Once he returns to fitness he will offer that same sharpness and direct running that Pochettino loves in his wingers, where he can hug the touchline, make runs from out to in, and punish teams.

He has the goal record to prove he is capable of being a gamechanger, too. With 138 goals for the club, nobody in the current squad has scored more, while the 30 goals he scored in the 2022/23 season are more than anyone else has ever managed in a single campaign at the club from the current squad.

That was a while ago, but at Barcelona he scored 14 goals and registered 14 assists last season, and he was in good form at the World Cup for England too, showing that he can deliver in the right setup. He was even described as 'frightening' by Ollie Watkins recently, showing how highly his teammates think of him.

"I can't speak highly enough of him," Watkins said on Rio Ferdinand Presents on YouTube.

"There was times (at Villa) where, like, I was on the bench, and he was keeping me out of the team, and I was frustrated, not because, not about him. It was just, I just wanted to play, but I could understand it, you know?

"There was times where, like, the PSG game, he absolutely ripped them apart. And I'm on the sideline, I'm thinking, I shouldn't come on there, really.

"Because he's, no, no, but I'm honest like that. Like, I don't have an ego. Like, I thought he was, because you read things and stuff like that, but I knew Marcus's talent, and I knew that the manager would get the best out of him. And to be fair, he did. And like, he was unbelievable. His ability is, yeah, it's frightening."

Pochettino will love what he can bring to the side, and he will love being trusted and relied upon. That could be the difference between getting him back to his best, and getting Man Utd back to where they feel they belong.

Threats to broadcasters hang over FCC nominee hearing

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

President Donald Trump’s Republican nominee to the Federal Communications Commission faced opposition Tuesday from Senate Democrats who fear the commission could be controlled exclusively by Republicans under Chairman Brendan Carr, who they vowed to investigate in the next Congress.

Appearing before the Senate Commerce Committee, nominee Danielle Thumann Severs limited her commitments to independence from Carr, including on regulating broadcasters based on their content decisions.

Much of the hearing focused on Carr and his threats to use the FCC to punish broadcasters he disagrees with.

Sen. Ben Ray Luján, D-N.M., said that he expects Carr will not last long in his position once Democrats take control of Congress in the new year.

“Mr. Carr, if you’re listening now, know you’re going to be in this body once a month, if not more, and in the House of Representatives as well, answering questions … so we can get to the bottom of the nonsense and the cover-ups that have been taking place,” Luján said.

The nomination of Thumann Severs, who currently serves as senior counsel to Carr, has led to consternation among Democrats due to continued vacancies at the FCC. The five-member commission is intended to have three members of the president’s party, including the chair, and two in the minority.

Currently Carr serves alongside fellow Republican Olivia Trusty and Democrat Anna M. Gomez, whose term expired earlier this year. Gomez may continue to serve through next year under the law if a replacement is not nominated and confirmed.

Democrats are concerned that if Thumann Severs were to be confirmed, the commission’s three Republican members could constitute a quorum on their own, allowing Trump to fire Gomez.

Sen. John Hickenlooper, D-Colo., said Senate precedent is to move pairs of one Democratic and one Republican nominee for the FCC and that he “strongly” believed the Thumann Severs nomination should have been paired with a renomination of Gomez in order to continue having “both parties at the table.”

“A bipartisan FCC is essential to avoid things like large-scale media consolidation [and] I think also, more fundamentally, to protect a free and diverse press [that] serves everyone,” Hickenlooper said.

In addition to urging Trump to renominate Gomez, Democrats underlined the need for a second Democratic member of the commission.

Hickenlooper highlighted Thumann Severs’ close work with Carr, who Democrats and some Republicans have been critical of for comments about using the FCC’s public interest standard against television stations airing programming Carr disagrees with.

Thumann Severs’ work at the FCC currently focuses on the Wireline, Public Safety and Homeland Security, and Consumer and Governmental Affairs bureaus, rather than the Media Bureau.

Committee Chair Ted Cruz, R-Texas, has also been critical of Carr’s comments. On Tuesday, he listed the FCC’s request for ABC-owned stations to submit early license renewals alongside earlier incidents in which he said Democrats encouraged the weaponization of the FCC against conservative voices.

“The First Amendment places important limits on the government’s ability to regulate speech,” Cruz said. “The FCC possesses significant regulatory authority over communications, so there is always a risk that it could weaponize its authority against Americans with whom it disagrees.”

Thumann Severs committed to Cruz that she would base licensing decisions on “the law and the record” and not use the FCC’s authority to “pressure or punish” broadcasters for protected speech.

However, when Sen. Edward J. Markey, D-Mass., questioned Thumann Severs on whether the FCC could punish broadcasters the president disagreed with, Thumann Severs repeated one of Carr’s arguments in discussing the FCC’s role.

“Broadcasters are subject to public interest obligations as part of the public trustee model in exchange for the licenses that they hold,” she said. “As part of those public interest obligations, they are subject to many different rules and requirements, and the FCC is charged with enforcing and investigating the public interest standard against broadcasters.”

Under repeated questions from Markey about Carr’s previous remarks, Thumann Severs said she would base her decisions “in the facts and the law and the record.”

Luján also asked Thumann Severs to name issues where she had disagreed with Carr or Trump. While she said her decisions would be her own, she declined to name any examples of disagreement.

In addition to questions about Carr, Democrats asked the nominee about the FCC’s E-Rate program, which provides low-cost connectivity for schools and libraries, and the Universal Service Fund, which funds the E-Rate program and other programs.

Thumann Severs said that she believes the E-Rate program has been successful and that the FCC has a statutory obligation to provide some kind of discounted service to schools and libraries. But she was less forthcoming on whether the FCC could shut down the program as it currently exists without congressional approval or whether the program should be restricted only to rural areas.

Late last month, Democratic members of the House Energy and Commerce Committee, led by Communications and Technology Subcommittee ranking member Doris Matsui, D-Calif., sent a letter to Senate Majority Leader John Thune, R-S.D., and Cruz urging Gomez’s renomination with any move to confirm a Republican nominee.

“Commissioner Gomez has shown the experience and judgment to continue that work,” the members wrote. “We therefore ask that the Senate pair any new Republican nominee with her renomination to a full term, consistent with the bipartisan design Congress built into this agency.”

On Monday, the Leadership Conference on Civil and Human Rights also sent a letter to committee leadership urging a delay on the hearing and any vote on Thumann Severs’ nomination until Gomez can be renominated.

“Independent agencies should not be extensions of presidential will: their leaders should exercise independent judgement based on their expertise and statutory mandates,” the group wrote.

Cruz did not indicate Tuesday when the committee might vote on Thumann Severs’ nomination, and no markup has yet been announced by the committee of hers’ and those of three others for two posts at NASA and one at the Transportation Department who also testified.

Cam Skattebo on Giants not adding a running back: They know what I can do

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Cam Skattebo is set to return to the Giants lineup for Thursday night’s game against the Commanders and he won’t be looking over his shoulder at any new additions to the team’s running back depth chart.

Fujino’s double sparks WCL comeback win for Manchester City against Inter

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Manchester City’s European campaign finally came roaring into life as they put four goals past Inter to record their first Women’s Champions League victory of the season and greatly improve their prospects of reaching the knockout stages.

The English side had to come from behind after a slow start but two goals from Aoba Fujino put them ahead before half-time, and second-half efforts from Kerstin Casparij and Grace Clinton gave them a 4-1 victory in Monza that doubled their points tally, after their draws against Bayern Munich, Real Madrid and Paris Saint-Germain.

The last two of those draws were frustrating results for Manchester City on home turf but they were far more fluid and clinical on the road, in the second half particularly, despite deploying a much-rotated team.

With more than half an eye on Saturday’s pivotal meeting with their domestic title rivals Chelsea, Andrée Jeglertz rested several of his key players, including their top-scorer Khadija ‘Bunny’ Shaw, the in-form winger Lauren Hemp and the ever-dependable midfield metronome Yui Hasegawa, all of whom were substitutes. They were joined on the bench by Rebecca Knaak, and therefore – with England’s Alex Greenwood missing the trip altogether with an ankle injury – Gracie Prior was handed her first Champions League appearance this season, starting at left-sided centre-back.

Prior was beaten in the air by the sheer speed and precision of Ivana Andrés’ pinpoint cross when Inter opened the scoring through Elisa Bartoli’s header, as the hosts took the lead somewhat against the run of play. Falling behind against an Inter team who have not conceded in any of their six Women’s Serie A matches this season was not an ideal situation for the English side to find themselves in, but they remained patient.

Chelsea were forced to settle for a point after being held to a goalless draw against Hacken in their Women’s Champions League clash.

The Blues had two goals disallowed in a frustrating first half in Gothenburg, while Jennifer Falk made some important stops throughout the contest. Sonia Bompastor's side remain unbeaten in the Champions League, but ultimately slipped to their second draw from four outings.

Wieke Kaptein headed home for Chelsea in the opening minutes, but the goal was chalked off when Lexi Potter was ruled to have fouled goalkeeper Falk.

Chelsea dominated possession and soon had the ball in the net again when Melvine Malard hit a low strike into the bottom corner, but the forward was marginally offside in the buildup.

Maika Hamano’s looping strike bounced off the crossbar during a late surge from Chelsea but Hacken defended their box well deep into stoppage time to clinch a valuable point.

In the other early game, Real Madrid beat Paris FC 3-0 with all the goals arriving in the second half including from substitutes Lineth Beerensteyn and Linda Caicedo. Madrid rose to sixth above Paris Saint-Germain and Bayern Munich, who faced each other later on Tuesday at Parc des Princes.

Roma defeated Servette 2-1 larer on Wednesday and Bayern Munich rose to second in the standings behind Barcelona by winning 2-1 at Paris Saint-Germain. The top three in the 18-team table at the start of play were Barcelona, OL Lyonnes and Arsenal who all play later in the week. Barcelona hosts Arsenal on Thursday. Agencies

The visitors took a while to click in attack but equalised nine minutes after going behind, when Fujino timed her run perfectly to latch on to Laura Blindkilde Brown’s classy, defence-splitting through ball. Fujino slotted in her second with ease from close range, just a minute before the end of the first half, on the rebound after a Mary Fowler effort was only half-cleared.

Despite having taken the lead, Jeglertz called upon two members of his cavalry – Hemp and Knaak – at half-time, continuing to manage other regular starters’ match minutes: Fujino was given a rest, along with Jade Rose.

Inter almost levelled three minutes after the restart but Ayaka Yamashita did superbly well to get a fingertip to Haley Bugeja’s bending effort, which looked destined for the bottom corner. The WSL champions were looking the stronger, though, aided by fine performances from Blindkilde Brown and Sam Coffey in midfield.

No topic is too small or too big for us to cover as we deliver a twice-weekly roundup of the wonderful world of women’s football

Coffey delivered a clever ball into the box to find Casparij through the middle and Casparij’s finish into the corner gave the English side a two-goal cushion. They began to turn on the style from then, spreading the ball into wide spaces much quicker than they had done earlier in the contest, embracing the lively movement of Hemp and Iman Beney.

Hemp, who had three goal contributions in her previous two appearances, dinked a delightful cross to the far post for Clinton to make it 4-1 from a tight angle, with Hemp’s delivery leaving the travelling fans purring. Her direct runs at the Inter right-back were a major worry for the hosts.

Yamashita pulled off her second world-class save of the night, this time a quick reaction save, to deny Michela Cambiaghi from close range, as Inter attempted to claw their way back into the match in the closing stages but City’s game-management was more than adequate as they calmly completed an important away victory. They will head to Stamford Bridge in a rich vein of form for one of the most important-looking WSL fixtures of the year.

Meta warns some of its AI glasses may get 'too warm' if they get wet

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

The company is offering free replacements to affected devices.

Meta is rolling out a software update for its AI glasses to fix an issue that causes some devices to become "too warm" when they get wet. The company describes it as a "rare issue" affecting only a "small number" of its glasses, but is nonetheless urging everyone with a pair of Meta Glasses to update their software and offering free replacements to those affected.

The company has begun notifying users about the forthcoming update which will roll out to everyone with a pair of Meta-branded frames, which includes Adventurer, Fury, Starfire, Nova and Capri models. Oakley and Ray-Ban glasses are not impacted, according to Meta.

While Meta doesn't use the word "overheating," the company compares its glasses fix to how smartphones automatically shut down when they get too hot. "We've implemented a software update to fix a rare issue that caused a small number of Meta Glasses to get too warm when moisture entered the device," a Meta spokesperson said in a statement. "It works similarly to a phone shutting off automatically when it needs to cool down. If people experience this, they can contact Meta Customer Service for a replacement."

In an email to users reported by 9to5Google, Meta says that the heat-related issue can be triggered by "excessive moisture or sweat" entering the glasses. That suggests that at least some of the company's own AI glasses may not be as water resistant as they're supposed to be. According to Meta, all of its AI glasses come with IPX4 water resistance which "means your glasses are safe if you get caught in light rain while you wear them."

The company hasn't indicated how many pairs of glasses might be affected, but says that those who experience the moisture-related shutdowns will be able to get a free replacement pair of glasses. Affected glasses will show a push notification if they do turn off unexpectedly, and the alert will direct them to Meta's customer support.

In the meantime, the company is urging everyone with a pair of Meta Glasses to update to the latest software, version 130, to ensure "the best performance." You can update your glasses from the Meta AI app by heading to Settings > Glasses > Updates. The version 130 update is still rolling out so it may take a day or so to hit everyone.

Have you experienced a pair of Meta Glasses getting too hot or unexpectedly shutting down? Contact the author at karissa [at] engadget.com or karissabe.50 on Signal.
