Why Ari Aster and Lars Knudsen’s Square Peg Built a Therapy Circle for Hollywood’s Next Generation in Texas

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Talent labs and mentorship programs are plentiful for aspiring filmmakers — but where do you turn when your career has already started, after your first movie or on the precipice of one, and especially when filmmaking can be such a lonely enterprise?

Enter Square Peg Social, the Austin, Texas-based filmmaking summit launched by Lars and Amy Knudsen and Ari Aster that offers four days of singular community-building: brutally honest advice, commiseration, and idea exchange shared among 35 participants, with the program now in its second year.

A weekend of panels, discussion, and camaraderie designed for filmmakers at the early stages of their career, this year’s talent-incubating event was even crashed by the likes of Zach Cregger and Richard Linklater — both of whom were down to get frank about their filmmaking highs and lows. (In fall 2025, IndieWire did a deep dive into Square Peg Social’s first year, just to show how much the event has already grown.) Related Stories ‘Ink’ Star Jack O’Connell on His ‘New Era’ as a Leading Man: ‘Thank F**k for Danny Boyle’ How Casting the New ‘Harry Potter’ Helped ‘Club Kid’ Find Its Adorable 10-Year-Old Star

But the program’s stable of 2026 mentors and panelists — i.e., filmmakers keen not only to open up to new filmmakers about their own struggles, but also to scout new talent — also counted “Primetime” and “You Can See Everything” director Lance Oppenheim, “Sing Sing” filmmaker Greg Kwedar, “The First Omen” director Arkasha Stevenson, and “Mother Mary” director David Lowery among them.

The closed-door, off-the-record, noncompetitive summit opened to applicants in May, later unfolding from September 17-21. Partners including the Austin Film Society, Belvedere, and Airbnb offset the cost, besides a modest application fee, for participants to hole up for four days and nights at Soho House that don’t promise job opportunities — but instead, the kind of lasting industry connections that can take years (and many starts and stops) to build. Also, no job opportunities isn’t entirely true. Take last year’s Square Peg Social mentee Yona Strauss, who’s now working for Knudsen and Aster’s Square Peg.

Just a sampling of this year’s participating producers and directors: producer Annie Huang, who worked on franchise IP like “Barbie” and “Wicked” at major studios; Jack Auen, co-founder of the filmmaking collective Cosmic Salon, which this year directed the New York critics’ favorite “Chronovisor”; Emmy-winning director of the Netflix short “The Turnaround,” Kyle Thrash; SXSW 2026 Neon Auteur Award winner Oliver Woolf; and many more who have their foot in the door, but are ready to cross the threshold.

IndieWire spoke to Lars Knudsen and Aster (who also co-founded the production company Square Peg, which this year backed “The Drama” and “Primetime”), plus Stevenson, Oppenheim, and Kwedar, along with three participants, to replay the on-the-ground experience in Austin. Late nights, early mornings, and vulnerabilities abounded across a long (but in some ways too short) weekend of intimate panels, small group sessions, and candid remarks from established filmmakers about the times they were trying to hold their own projects together with Scotch tape and paper clips. Think of it like a mini summer camp devoted to obsessive ambition.

“When you’ve been doing it for this long, sometimes it feels like you’re just a little bit going through the motions. You’re going from movie to movie to movie, and you sometimes forget why we do this thing that we do. We’re in this world working with these artists where they don’t have a plan B. Having now worked with 30, 40 filmmakers, I realized that when my wife brought up the idea of doing something because she has her own aversions to the industry, and that there isn’t really — it sounds cliché — but there just isn’t a lot of kindness or compassion or openness in our industry.

“There’s a lot of fear, there’s a lot of rejection. We wanted to create a space for filmmakers where there’s radical transparency and where it was truly, truly focused on a selfless thing: helping young filmmakers navigate the industry at a time in their career where things can either take off or not, and really demystifying the process of making movies, especially during the stage where they’re often in the back seat, which is financing or casting. Because for all these filmmakers that I work with and all the filmmakers that we’ve invited there, even the producers, it’s the first thing to think about when they wake up and the last thing to think about when they go to bed. Nothing is more important. I wanted to create an event where that is treated with the utmost importance, almost like it’s a life-or-death thing because it is all they want to do.”

“When Lars and Amy first broached the idea with me, I realized that it was something that I wish I had when I got out of school [at AFI], because I really did have to navigate that on my own. I was making a lot of shorts with whatever money I could raise and writing screenplays and trying to get one screenplay going. When that failed, writing something else and really looking for any sort of mentorship or guidance, It’s a very demoralizing process, and it was very, very hard for me to find traction, and it hasn’t gotten easier. It’s as bad as it’s ever been. There’s just so much uncertainty right now in the industry, not just for filmmakers. Even the lives of these studios are suddenly in danger and in question.

“There are a lot of filmmakers that Lars and I have been championing and films that we’ve been trying to get financing for that have proven almost impossible… It felt like a good thing to create a space where filmmakers can come together and commiserate and share experiences and connect. More important than even the connections that are made between the mentors and the mentees here are actually the relationships made among the people who are attending. A lot of them end up working together because we have producers and directors coming in. Last year proved really successful, and I would just say it was refreshing for us, too, that it galvanized us to keep doing this. Because last year it was almost an experiment: Will this work? How will we feel having done this? … Everybody walked away feeling good and feeling like it was a really useful and positive experience.”

“I’ve never really had the opportunity to just hang out with so many great filmmakers, not only just working filmmakers whose work I’ve loved, like David Lowery, who I’ve idolized for so long, but there’s so many filmmakers that were actually in the program as participants whose work I also have really admired. So having a space where we could all just kind of talk and vent and really nerd out on stuff that you never really get the opportunity to do.

“Because this was so filmmaker-driven and filmmaker-led, it encouraged the spirit of vulnerability [where] everyone really went there. I felt like maybe I should go back to therapy or something, but I felt like this was a great opportunity to therapize and also dump all of my feelings into a forum where you could just be open and honest about the process of making films… Most people had made features before, and were in the process of making features. It felt much more like we were at a film festival and everyone was able to connect with each other in a much deeper way, more than anything I’ve been to before.

“The first night I was there, I stayed up till like three in the morning, and I went on one of those electric scooters, the limes with Lars and Christian [Vasquez], my producer, and a producer from the event, too. We went to Whataburger and just kept talking. I think that was sort of the vibe. I don’t know. I guess everyone was encouraged to hang out for as long as they wanted to and to kick back and relax. There was another night we closed down the Soho House lounge bar, and Amy was like, ‘Let’s keep hanging out.’ So we were outside the bar, and everybody pulled together all of the drinks in their mini bar, and we created an ad hoc bar outside of the Soho House, and we kept drinking and hanging.”

“I don’t think you can really do film if you have a plan B because there’s always an escape hatch, and film requires every single atom of your body, mind and soul. It naturally becomes a very therapeutic place. I think because we’re all in this constant state of obsession, that can get really tiring, and it can get really lonely. … [The industry] attracts very sensitive souls. [With Square Peg Social] I didn’t even realize how badly I needed it until the first year. I’d known Amy and Lars before that, and Amy said that she wanted to start a Social. … I thought it would be much smaller, smaller. It ended up being this great big thing where, yeah, it does feel like a therapy circle, really.

“Everyone was really struck by the safety of the event. There was this unspoken agreement that everybody would be checking your egos at the door, and that this would be a very safe place to share your personal vulnerabilities, which is exceptionally rare in film because resources are so limited, and it’s just naturally a very competitive industry. Letting yourself be very, very vulnerable in front of your peers is something you were, in a sense, trained not to do but sometimes desperately need. At Square Peg, also, the line between mentee and mentor blurs quite a bit, which is why I think it’s so beneficial for everybody who goes there.

“It kind of reminds me of summer camp a little bit because you’re up until two or three in the morning talking to people, and then you’re up at eight again.”

“I always think of this term, like, whenever you take an elevator up, if you get to the top floor, send it back down for someone else to get on. [Square Peg] is one of the busiest production companies working today. To dedicate this weekend for emerging artists to have a forum where they can come and feel completely comfortable being very vulnerable with their ambitions, but also the things that they’re afraid of, and to hear that echoed from industry mentors who are being just as vulnerable about their ambitions and the things that they struggle with and the heartbreaks they’ve had, it’s an amazing table that’s set.

“I had ulterior motives in the, in the sense that Clint [Bentley] and I have started this new company, Ethos, and we have a deal at Netflix to finance films from emerging artists, and so I was also there … to look for talent. We’ll be following up with a lot of these artists to just hear more about the films they want to make. One thing that I appreciated about it was that it wasn’t like a pitch fest. You’re not there to pitch your project. You’re there to soak it in and learn.

“It had this healthy blend of these panels, and not the normal kind of panel. One was a panel of filmmakers led off by Ari and Lars, telling a very, very brutally honest story of how hard one of their films was to make. That kind of created a pathway for how the rest of the weekend would unfold.”

“I think the whole reason anyone works in film is that we were once kids making videos in the woods with our friends, making with a shitty camera. And there’s nothing else in this world, and I think I probably speak for everyone, where I can’t imagine film is the only thing that’s our main passion and hobby and something we can also make a career out of. So there was never a plan B at all. Whenever someone asked me, ‘What do you think you’d do if you couldn’t do anything film-adjacent?’ I have literally no idea, maybe a teacher or something. That’s super nice that Lars and the rest of Square Peg was saying that there is no plan B. There’s only: If it takes longer to get to Plan A, then it takes longer to get to Plan A.

“I remember on the very first day before we even properly began, Lars did a little speech with Ari, and Lars was like, ‘We’re going to be 100 percent transparent with you guys.’ And I remember thinking, ‘Oh, that’s cool, but I wonder how transparent.’ And by the end of it, I was like, ‘Fuck me, that was transparent.’ There really was nothing off the table.”

“It was definitely something that I’ve never experienced before in this industry, to experience that level of candor and to hear these traumatic experiences that they have gone through in not a sensationalistic way, but almost in a very personal and almost relatable way, despite me having never experienced that level of millions of dollars of movie budget making — but on a personal level of how the heartbreaks had felt for them individually and the hardships of filmmaking. Coming into this program, there are so many people within our cohort who are incredibly talented, who have accomplished so many things. And coming into it, oftentimes you’ll have a fear of: ‘OK, how do I compare in relation to their accomplishments?’ But in hearing Lars and Ari talk about their own hardships, I think that’s what influenced me to really share some of mine coming up in the industry and producing independently.

“Some of the traumatic experiences that I have also faced are ones that I feel like I have to not talk about or hide from people, for fear of them looking like mistakes or of me looking like I’m not good enough to be doing this. And we were able to share that, like Ollie [Woolf] and I, in our own little producer’s therapy session with the mentors, with Lars. That was really healing for me.”

“[Ari, Lars, and the mentors are] on, not a different level, but they’re all going through similar issues and this and that, whether it’s a low budget or a higher budget type of ordeal. Everyone’s going through the same thing in their own way, in a sense. Just to know the people that I’ve been looking up to for a very long time, the reason in large part why I got into making films, to know that they’re going through similar things and just keep going, and it’s fine. No matter what level we’re going to be at, there’s still something to navigate. So I thought it was very cool to see, over the course of five days, to hear these individual experiences and stuff and how you could relate it back to what we’re going through right now and where we are.

“It was information overload in the best way possible. At a certain point, I hope I’m retaining everything that was said over those five days because it was nonstop, all useful information. I wasn’t even taking notes at a certain point because I’m like, ‘I can’t write all this stuff down. I’m just going to try and soak it all in.'”

Walmart’s 2026 Black Friday and Cyber Monday Sales: Dates, Early Deals, and What to Expect

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

If you're thinking of picking up any big-ticket items soon—whether for yourself or as a gift for someone on your list— I would advise you to wait until you see what retailers have in store for the holiday sales season, which will kick off in earnest in mid-November. Walmart is the first major retailer to announce its Black Friday and Cyber Monday plan for 2026, and they actually involve three major shopping events between now and the December holidays.

As it did in 2025, Walmart will hold three sales between Nov. 12 and Dec. 2, as outlined in a recent press release. The sales will be both online and in-store, and will target a mix of Walmart+ members and general shoppers.

The events will take place on three different dates:

Walmart's Black Friday Deals Event 1: Nov. 12–15

Available online and in stores starting at 6 a.m. local time

Walmart+ members get six hours of early access beginning online at 12:01 a.m. ET on November 12

Walmart's Black Friday Deals Event 2: Nov. 23–29

Online only: Nov. 23 starting at 12:01 a.m. ET

Online and in stores: Nov. 27 starting at 6 a.m. local time

Walmart+ members get six hours of early access online beginning Nov. 23 at 12:01 a.m. ET

Walmart's Cyber Monday Deals Event: Nov. 30-Dec. 2

Walmart's physical stores will open at 6:00 a.m. local time on November 27 for their Black Friday events. Of course, they will start online earlier, so make sure to check their website as well.

Walmart says that you can expect discounts on electronics, toys, fashion, beauty, home goods, gifts, and everyday essentials. Although the company doesn't provide much in the way of specifics in its press release, last year's sales included discounts on TCL TVs, Dyson vacuums, Dreo appliances, and toys from LEGO.

No. But if you are a Walmart+ member, you'll get early access to the sale. You can sign up for a free 30-day Walmart+ subscription and cancel it afterwards or get the annual plan for $98 ($8.17/month).

Deals will be both online and in the physical stores. There might be deals online that you won't see in-stores and vice versa.

The holiday season will see major sales from the same list of retailers that compete for your dollars throughout the year—namely, Best Buy, Target, and Amazon. Each will have its own version of Black Friday and Cyber Monday sales. So far, only Walmart has revealed its plans.

In short, yes. Though they aren't quite as impactful as they once were, Black Friday and Cyber Monday sales offer discounts that can be rare throughout the rest of the year. Whether it's a major purchase you've been saving up for or you’re shopping for gifts, it’s a good time to look for discounts, especially on items like appliances and electronics. Last year's sales included categories such as tech, home improvement, and fitness.

Black Friday used to be when major retailers offered their best deals on expensive tech and appliances, while Cyber Monday was for cheaper tech and gave smaller businesses a chance to compete online. Now, however, the distinction is almost meaningless: Every major retailer offers sales on both days, which means the smart move is to know what you want, use price trackers to monitor the deals, and refer to our Black Friday coverage, where we do all the searching, curating, and price tracking for you.

Report: Liverpool enter transfer battle with Barcelona for €25m star

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Liverpool have been linked with a whole host of players ahead of the winter transfer window.

Many areas among their ranks need upgrading and, even though fans knew this during the summer, Liverpool still only signed four new players.

And, one of them (Jeremy Jacquet) was already confirmed all the way back in January.

Now that we're a few months into the season, it's clear that some cracks are starting to show among the Reds' ranks.

So, with the January transfer window now edging closer, the rumour mill is likely to go into overdrive.

Liverpool and Barcelona have both set their eyes on Juventus' Andrea Cambiaso

Liverpool's full-backs have been a major problem for them this term.

Left-back has been the main issue but, there have been times when some dodgy situations have occurred at right-back.

Indeed, Ronald Araujo has performed much better than Kostas Tsimikas or Milos Kerkez have all season but, it still feels like an upgrade could be beneficial, or, at least someone with a bit of a higher ceiling than the Uruguayan needs to be brought in.

Because, while the defender is good, he's going to be 28 years old by June 2027 which is usually when the performance levels of players start to drop.

So, that's why this latest news from TuttoJuve should pique the interest of fans.

"Barcelona aren't the only team to have included Cambiaso on their list of targets," they wrote.

"Liverpool and Manchester United have also reportedly expressed interest in the Italian full-back, who is particularly appreciated for his ability to cover multiple roles on the wings.

"In particular, Manchester United are reportedly evaluating reinforcements for the left side of defence, while Liverpool are reportedly continuing to monitor various profiles to broaden their options.

"Cambiaso's versatility, which allows him to play on both flanks and in different playing systems, represents a particularly attractive characteristic for major European clubs.

"But one question remains: would €20-25 million be enough to convince Juventus to give up on a player with these characteristics?"

Andrea Cambiaso's versatility is a major selling point for the Italian

Versatility has become a key part of football in recent years.

Clubs now need players to be able to play in multiple positions if needed.

Because, with the sheer number of injuries that they suffer, having just one back-up option for each position isn't really enough anymore.

Cambiaso has been a regular feature for Juventus over the last few years and his ability to play on either side of the pitch is massively beneficial to any club that signs him.

The Italy international is also only 26 years old so, while he already has a lot of experience under his belt, there is still room for him to improve.

Right now, it's unclear what the future holds for Cambiaso but, if Liverpool are serious about reigniting their interest in him, they're going to have to be quick, especially with Barcelona lurking.

The VR Disney Plus app on Meta Quest now has 3D movies

With the updated Disney Plus Quest app, you’ll be able to stream more than 60 3D movies and watch films in immersive virtual environments.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

The 3D movies you can check out include The Avengers and Black Panther, Meta says. But the updated Quest app doesn’t just offer 3D movies; you’ll also be able to see some films in themed, immersive virtual environments, like a Tatooine environment when watching Star Wars: A New Hope or a Scare Floor one for Monsters, Inc. I got to see an Avengers-themed immersive environment for myself while trying Meta’s forthcoming VR Glasses in September, and while it was novel for the few minutes I tried it, I wasn’t sure if it would be good during an entire movie.

The Disney Plus app on the VR Glasses, which are set to release sometime in spring 2027, will also let you watch IMAX Enhanced movies, including Avengers: Endgame, Lightyear, and Tron: Ares. The VR Glasses will also be a place to watch 3D movies from Lionsgate.

REI vs. Dick’s Sporting Goods Black Friday Deals, Which Is Better?

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

The two brands are known for wilding out on sales, but here’s how to find the best deals.

We earn a commission for products purchased through some links in this article. Why Trust Us?

Your cart has been sitting there since October, and I get it. I’ve got a few (hundred) tabs open myself. As someone with many marathons, a few ultras, and more halfs and 5Ks than I can count, I can tell you the real Black Friday question isn’t whether to buy shoes and running gear; it’s where. REI and Dick’s are two of the names that come up the most often, and they handle the holiday very differently. REI is again giving its employees a paid day off on Friday, November 27, so its deals land early. Dick’s has typically stretched its sale across the weekend. Deciding who has the best deals comes down to these two questions: What are you actually shopping for, and how much do you need? Here’s how the deals stack up.

One thing both brands are doing, and doing pretty well, is taking some big-name running shoes and discounting them hard. Think Hoka, Asics, and Brooks top trainers with discounts of $50 or more, with deeper discounts on previous years’ models. The deals don’t match model to model, and each outlet has different shoes at different prices. For example, REI offers steeper discounts on Hoka, and Dick’s has better pricing on brands like Adidas.

The Hoka Bondi 9 is a favorite among distance runners, especially those with flatter feet. It has a supercritical foam midsole that makes it light and responsive, with a snug fit, a generously padded collar, and comfortable, max-stack cushioning. It’s a big improvement over the Bondis of antiquity, and it’s one of their most popular shoes overall. And the price difference between REI and Dick’s is significant, with REI the clear winner.

Can’t go wrong with the ultra-comfortable Adizero Evo SL from Adidas. It’s fun to run in; it’s bouncy, responsive, and keeps you happy on long runs. Dubbed by Jeff Dengate as the Shoe of the Year for 2025, this trainer remains one of our most beloved shoes of the past few years.

The On Cloudmonster 3 is the perfect mix of firm and cushioned, keeping you supported on your longest efforts, whether walking or running. It’s a max-cushioned trainer with On’s CloudTec technology that compresses and rebounds with every strike, making it responsive, a bit bouncy, and ready for high mileage.

REI is also known for its Co-op line of gear. It’s their own, high-quality gear that comes in at terrific prices. For Black Friday and Cyber Monday, many of those items are discounted even further, making it hard to say no to already affordable goodies. REI also offers a variety of smaller brands like Rabbit, which they are able to offer on sale. If you’re into smaller brands, or you’re a big fan of Co-op, REI is the place to hit up.

Rabbit makes great UPF-rated long sleeves that are perfect for layering on cooler days or staying cool in the sun on hotter days. This one has one of my favorite features—a watch panel that lets you wear the shirt over your hands with thumb holes, so you can still see your watch. The fabric is buttery, the seams are sealed, so they’re not itchy or irritating, and they launder like a dream.

REI makes really good hydration vests, often in a more expansive size range than other brands. I’ve tested this one, and I like it a lot. It has space for a bladder and two flasks, plus room for snacks, gear, and whatever you happen to take off during your run because you’ve overheated.

Some of the biggest sales at Dick’s Sporting Goods are on its biggest-ticket items like treadmills, running watches, and other exercise equipment. These are categories that REI doesn’t touch as much, and some not at all (treadmills). This area is where Dick’s really shines. You could get hundreds off this time of year, and you should take full advantage.

This is truly one of my favorite “get-up-and-run” treadmills, and for 40 percent off, it’s tripping into “cheap” treadmill territory. It’s a solid treadmill with a good-size deck, plenty of connectivity, incline, and speed, all at a fabulous price tag.

Suunto has improved so much over the past few years, and this is one of the best examples. It’s a dynamic, multisport running watch with terrific GPS, battery life, connectivity, and a family of apps to help keep you connected. It’s also one of the better-looking running watches available, and while that might not be the most important thing, it’s still worth considering.

Both brands have fantastic Black Friday discounts, but they look pretty different. Dick’s has the upper hand on big-ticket items, but their shoes are a bit more expensive. REI wins on shoe price, and in the smaller labels and private-label brands they’re known for. And both stores carry shoe brands the other doesn't. In the end, it’s about picking your faves and knowing what you’re shopping for.

Cat Bowen, senior editor of commerce; reviews, is a seasoned runner with more than 20 years of distance running experience, including dozens of marathons, half marathons, and even a few ultra marathons. For over a decade, she has tested parenting, fitness, home, and running gear and written in-depth guides to help readers with their next purchase. Holding multiple advanced degrees and currently studying kinesiology, Cat Bowen brings research-backed insight to all of her guides. Passionate about women’s health and neurodivergent inclusion, she advocates for closing research gaps and helping others—especially AudHD people—find joy in running and fitness.

Bucchioni: “Napoli? Allegri è un allenatore in difficoltà, la squadra è usurata”

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Enzo Bucchioni, giornalista, ha parlato del Napoli negli studi di Sportitalia: “Va analizzata sotto l’aspetto psicologico. Allegri è un allenatore in difficoltà, si vede anche dalla postura del corpo. Lui lo sa benissimo che ha sulle spalle l’esonero dalla Juventus, il brutale esonero dal Milan, che non ha ancora digerito e che ha dimostrato di avere ancora dentro di sè nello sfogo dopo la Roma. Ha tirato in ballo, guarda caso, il Milan, perché è qualcosa che si porta ancora dentro. Va nello spogliatoio, gli succede un’altra cosa, viene contestato e cosa dice a se stesso? «Sono ancora il più forte». Quasi come se volesse sentirselo dire da fuori, ma se lo dice da solo per caricarsi. un posto dopo quell’esonero che comunque gli brucia ancora sulla pelle. Questa è la prima cosa. Dal punto di vista calcistico, torno a quello che diceva Credendino, perché tutto è nato da lì. Quando Antonio Conte mi dice: «Io non parteciperò a un funerale, non accompagno il morto», quella frase andava analizzata, non come una battuta sul fatto che Conte frequenta un ristorante da cento euro, come fece… Lui ha voluto dire una cosa ben precisa: questa squadra è usurata. E’ usurata da due Scudetti, molti giocatori sono gli stessi, è usurata dal punto di vista fisico, è vecchia e andava cambiata profondamente”.

Mrs. Gallery Marks 10 Years of Getting Art Collectors to Queens

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

A decade since Sara Maria Salamone and Tyler Lafreniere found their storefront space on Craigslist, the gallery presents a rotating anniversary show that channels its evolving nature.

Maspeth has not traditionally been thought of as an arts hub like Bushwick or the East Village, but one gallery has thrived by drawing collectors from all over the world to Western Queens.

“We’ve had people stop here on the way to the airport and end up buying something,” Mrs. Associate Director Lily Frances Pendery told Hyperallergic. “We had an advisor who recently spent an hour and a half with us. Anyone who comes in the door is serious about what their visit is for.”

From its leafy one-way street near the Long Island Expressway, Mrs. has worked with dozens of emerging artists, held 72 shows, and traveled to art fairs in Paris, South Korea, and across the country. This fall, the gallery is celebrating its anniversary with DECENNIUM: Ten Years at Mrs., a rotating exhibition of 30 works by 23 artists.

Create a free account to read more articles each month, and get the best of Hyperallergic in your inbox.

This residency at the U-M Stamps School, in partnership with the U-M Museum of Art (UMMA), offers artists a $20,000 honorarium, housing, studio space, and a culminating exhibition in UMMA’s prominent Stenn Gallery.

The Bayeux tapestry, a new biography of Edward Hopper, The Met's exhibition catalogue on Krasner and Pollock, what architecture is, and more

Funding for tuition, healthcare, and stipends is available. Apply to the University of Maryland, Baltimore County program by Feb 1 to be considered for financial support.

Generously funded, renowned faculty and visiting artists, spacious studios, and an exhibition at the Manetti Shrem Museum of Art.

My Fall Outfits Were Feeling Stale—12 November Outfit Ideas I’m Trying Instead

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Now that the weather is cooling off, leaving the house involves grappling with the internal struggle: will I be cute or will I be comfortable today? On chilly days, the heaviest coats or most weather-proof shoes are all-too-appealing, but they don’t always show off your style in the way you might hope. Luckily, this month there are some clever ways you can meet in the sartorial middle: ensuring you don’t freeze, but still putting your outfit first.

With darker evenings here, football season in full swing, and Thanksgiving around the corner, think of these 12 November outfits as the puzzle pieces to help you check the boxes for everything coming up on your calendar. Playing with bold colors, contrasting textures, and even sneakily comfy silhouettes are some of the simple ways you can make November your best-dressed month of the entire year. Check out some of my favorite looks below.

An on-trend striped rugby sweater is the kind of dress-up-or-down staple you might usually style with jeans and sneakers. But for any dressy events on the calendar, try playing with texture and structure by styling it with a full, sequin skirt. It’s a look that reads dressy without trying too hard.

One way to make comfy pieces like oversized knits and track pants feel instantly dressy? By going bold with color. For days when you want to look polished but will mostly be relaxing on the couch, this is a great place to start.

It’s prime football season, which might mean your Sunday afternoon is devoted to keeping up with your favorite team. A pair of relaxed jeans and a suede jacket is a combination that’s cute enough to wear to brunch with your girlfriends, but won’t feel out of place at a sporty party.

If you’re the type to dress up for Thanksgiving (or any occasion really), a trim satin suit is a dressy look in stark contrast to the usual oversized staples. The shiny fabric gives the look a festive touch, while styled back to a simple black top and shoes, the overall effect is very ‘90s Prada—who can complain about that?

Odds are you’ll be headed out of town to spend some time with family. Your travel day look should always put comfort first, but a few small styling choices can make the outfit feel purposeful instead of schlubby. A crew neck tee paired with a V-neck sweater, a trench coat, and chunky Dad sneakers are what make Zoë Kravitz’ outfit look so good.

As winter approaches, there’s something enduringly chic about a white-on-white look. Even if your outfit is as simple as jeans and an oversized sweater, the matchiness pulls everything together. Black accessories are an easy pick, but you could also add a pop of contrast by styling the outfit with red shoes.

Call it genius, call it sneaky; there’s no need to wear full-on sweats to the Thanksgiving table. A dressy matching set can be just as comfortable. Look for styles with a soft, stretchy plissé fabric, which has a dressed-up, flowy air but feels as easy as your coziest loungewear.

This time of year, fashion is all about rich, sumptuous textures and hues. Gigi Hadid’s party look puts a soft, check flannel skirt in contrast with leather boots and a matching jacket, and the effect is greater than the sum of its parts. Cozy yet chic.

When you don’t want to think too hard about your look, an all-black outfit is an easy fallback with polish. Try opting for an oversized knit, styled with tailored trousers and loafers for a look you could wear to the office, but would feel just as comfortable in for a Saturday spent shopping with your most fashionable friends.

Breaking up a tailored look with a cummerbund or even a scarf can be an easy way to repurpose pieces you might usually relegate to the office. This dressy yet comfortable look is versatile, so throw it on for post-work martinis or even a night at the orchestra.

Fringe is a trend to know this season, and Nara Smith’s Kallmeyer dress is the blueprint for a festive Thanksgiving party or wedding when you want to dress to impress. The fringe detail adds movement, which makes it ideal for a situation with a dance floor. Layer a leather jacket or a black trench coat on top for your time in transit.

A great pair of jeans should always be in heavy rotation, but how you style them is an easy way to make them feel fresh. Here, mixing feminine pieces with more classic menswear details gives this a casual-yet-effortful effect. Try a blazer and little heels with socks for a playful spin on pieces you likely already have sitting in your closet.

Aemilia Madden is a freelance writer, editor, and consultant with over 10 years of experience in the fashion industry. She’s written for publications including Vogue, Harper’s Bazaar, Elle,The Cut, and The Wall Street Journal.

She also writes a newsletter called Taeste Bud, that covers stuff we buy, places we go, and the people we want to be. Previously, she held roles at The Zoe Report, WhoWhatWear and Popsuagar. Aemilia attended the University of California, Berkeley where she majored in Political Economics. In her time away from the internet, Aemilia is probably out on a hike.

The Trendiest Fragrance From the Year You Graduated School

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

From ’80s spicy florals to today’s gourmand favorites, these are the top scents that defined decades

It almost goes without saying that what’s buzzy in beauty often sends ripples through culture, inspiring a flood of viral social media videos and a rise in consumer spending. While there will always be discussion around the most popular skincare treatments, makeup ideas, and hair looks to date, scent's elusiveness makes it hard to pin down—so we’re taking a closer look at some of the trendiest fragrances from the mid-’80s through the present day.

Our curation of celebrated scents, like Calvin Klein’s CK One eau de toilette, Le Labo’s Santal 33 eau de parfum, and more, is not only a walk down memory lane, but also an accounting of the accords that have lived rent-free in our minds for decades. Though technically invisible, these enduring, dynamic fragrances deserve a spot on any beauty holy grail list. Keep reading for the trendiest perfumes, eaux de toilette, and body mists Bazaar editors have loved over the decades.

Dior’s ’80s-born Poison eau de toilette combines honey and musk accords for a sexy, seductive scent. The warm fragrance has long-lasting sillage and a spicy edge, making it perfect for date night.

Key notes: plum, wild berries, coriander, anise, Brazilian rosewood, tuberose, white honey, cinnamon, incense, carnation, jasmine, rose, orange blossom, vanilla, amber, sandalwood, musk, heliotrope, vetiver, cedarwood

This fresh, floral fragrance from Calvin Klein aims to bottle romance, thanks to a bouquet of notes like freesia, white lily, violet, and rose. Eternity’s iconic campaign was fronted by supermodel Christy Turlington, and the fragrance name itself was inspired by a diamond wedding band once owned by the Duke of Windsor, inscribed with the word “Eternity.”

Key notes: freesia, mandarin, green notes, sage, white lily, marigold, muguet, narcissus, carnation, violet, rose, jasmine, sandalwood, patchouli, amber, musk, heliotrope

If you ever visited Elizabeth Arden’s Red Door spa when the brand was in business, you probably recognize the distinctive color and design of this beloved perfume. Inspired by the ethos of beloved locations nationwide, this popular scent captures the destination spa's grandeur and elegance in its heyday through a bold, floral lens.

Key notes: freesia, red roses, orchids, honey, sandalwood

This star-shaped bottle is almost as iconic as the refillable scent inside. An addictive combination of fruit-forward and gourmand notes, Thierry Mugler’s renowned fragrance is warm and spicy with a unique celestial appeal.Key notes: bergamot, red fruits, patchouli

Elizabeth Taylor was a trendsetter in more ways than one, including the art of fragrance-making. White Diamonds is one of the most noteworthy celebrity brands of all time, and was inspired by the actress’s love of—what else—diamonds, along with flowers and lush gardens.Key notes: Amazon lily, neroli, bergamot, orange, Egyptian tuberose, jasmine, ylang-ylang, Turkish rose, carnation, oak moss, sandalwood, amber, musk, patchouli

Firmenich senior perfumer Clement Gavarry smelled CK One as a teenager and instantly fell in love. “This fragrance was revolutionary and the first gender-neutral fragrance with a construction that, for me, has everlasting freshness, a beautiful trail, and is memorable,” he says. “It captures all of the aspects that make a scent a classic—to this day, it is a modern fragrance that stands in its own category. Truly timeless.”

Tommy Hilfiger was one of the most beloved fashion brands of the ’90s, so it’s no surprise its buzzy Tommy Girl scent is considered one of the most popular as well. The clean, fresh fragrance lends itself to spring and summer months, since its combination of citrus, mint, and green-floral notes is like a breath of fresh air.

Key notes: bergamot, spearmint, jasmine, rose, iced tea accord, sandalwood, amber accord, musk

Clinique’s Happy eau de toilette was a certified hit in the ’90s thanks to its fruity-floral blend that evokes positivity and happiness. The brand even commissioned a third-party neurosensory study to measure fragrance wearers’ conscious and subconscious emotional responses, which found that 97 percent of people feel happy when they smell Happy.

Key notes: ruby red grapefruit, mandarin, bergamot, Hawaiian wedding flower, morning dew orchid, mimosa, transparent wood accord

You might be inspired to dream up a fairy-tale date night or give yourself a little extra self-love after spritzing on this themed fragrance from Ralph Lauren. It opens with top notes of pink pepper and mandarin orange before transitioning to a woodier finish, courtesy of musk and patchouli.

Actress Charlize Theron was one of the muses behind this generational Dior scent for 20 years before Rihanna recently took the reins in 2024. But its celebrity appeal is only one of the reasons J’adore deserves a spot on our list. The enduring warm, fruity-floral perfume was a bestseller shortly after its 1999 release and continues to be a must-have for beauty lovers and Bazaar editors alike.

Key notes: ylang-ylang, Damascus rose, jasmine sambac, jasmine grandiflorum, pear, candied plum, violet, lily-of-the-valley

If you’ve ever owned or been a superfan of Victoria’s Secret’s Love Spell, you’re likely a millennial with superior taste in fragrance mists. Originally launched as part of the brand’s Garden collection, its fruity-floral blend is playful and light with plenty of staying power.

Key notes: red apple, cherry blossom, peach, lily-of-the-valley, jasmine, lilac, airy musk, blonde woods, tamarind

Originally launched in 2001 as an eau de toilette, Dolce&Gabbana’s Light Blue makes it easy to mentally transport yourself abroad to the Mediterranean. The fresh, citrusy scent is easy to layer and beautifully bottles the aquatic essence of the Amalfi Coast.

Key notes: Sicilian lemon, apple, cedarwood

Several adored iterations of Chanel’s Chance eau de parfum exist in 2026, but the original 2002 version set the stage for its now-legendary status in the fragrance world. Beyond its singular rounded bottle, which sets it apart from other prestige scents, Chance’s blend of pink pepper, jasmine absolute, and amber patchouli makes it unforgettable.

Key notes: pink pepper, jasmine absolute, amber patchouli, white musk, vanilla

I personally was borderline obsessed with my bottle of Glow by J.Lo eau de toilette when it first launched in 2002. Not only is its Y2K-coded bottle glamorous, with layered nameplate necklaces, but the floral, musky scent strikes the perfect balance between soapy and powdery without being cloying.

Key notes: orange flower, grapefruit, citrus, rose, sandalwood, amber, jasmine, vanilla, musk, orris

This Y2K fragrance truly smells as delicious as its name implies, with an apple top note leading the perfume as a nod to its origins in New York City. The fresh, floral, and fruity scent doesn’t take itself too seriously, which is part of its playful, whimsical appeal.

Viktor&Rolf’s Flowerbomb eau de parfum isn’t just for floral fragrance aficionados. The trendy scent, first launched in 2005, is grounded by woody notes of patchouli, amber, and musk that round out its feminine accords.

Key notes: peony accord, centifolia, rose, freesia accord

Break out your matching Juicy Couture tracksuit! This popular fragrance from Juicy Couture is made to make your mouth water, combining notes of watermelon, passion fruit, and crème brûlée for a fruity-gourmand scent housed in a bottle that doesn’t shy away from early 2000s glam.

Key notes: watermelon, passion fruit, crème brûlée notes

Bright Crystal eau de parfum is one of Versace’s early fragrance gems, with a fresh floral composition that stands the test of time. The perfume is subtle enough for daily wear, but also impactful enough to make a serious scent statement.

Key notes: pomegranate, yuzu iced accord, vegetal amber, acajou, musk

Another buzzy fragrance with a bottle design as iconic as the scent itself, Marc Jacobs’s Daisy is inspired by innocence and purity. Notes of wild strawberry and violet petals, along with white accords of birch and cedarwood, give the fragrance its unique floral-woody musk. Daisy won the Fragrance Foundation’s FiFi Awards for Women’s Luxe Fragrance of the Year and Women’s Prestige Best Packaging the year after its launch, in 2008.

Key notes: wild strawberry, velvety violet petals, gardenia, jasmine, birch cedarwood

All of fashion’s It girls were reaching for Byredo’s Gypsy Water eau de parfum in 2008, and for good reason. The free-spirited scent is as woody as it is adventurous, with a subtle, balanced—and almost skinlike—finish, an ode to the nomadic customs of Romani culture.

Key notes: bergamot, lemon, juniper berries, pepper, incense, soft iris, pine needle, amber, sandalwood, vanilla

After 15 seasons, the hit reality series Love & Hip-Hop is coming to an end in 2026. However, before it ends, a new six-part limited reality TV series titled Love & Hip Hop: The Final Chapter drops on cable networks VH1, as well as MTV and BET. Explore Billboard See latest videos, charts and news

The series features cast members from all four cities from the spinoffs, such as New York, Atlanta, Hollywood and Miami. It has new interviews with Stevie J, Joseline Hernandez, Yandy Smith, Mimi Faust, Rasheeda Frost, Trick Daddy, Safaree Samuels, Trina, Ray J, Scrappy and Yung Joc, along with Angela Yee, Nina Parker and Jess Hilarious. It’s available to access with a subscription to Philo, which is the “home of reality TV online” and free to watch with a seven-day free trial.

Love & Hip Hop: The Final Chapter broadcasts starting on Tuesday (Nov. 10) with a start time of 8 p.m. ET/PT. Each episode is 42 minutes long, while new episodes drop every Tuesday until Dec. 15.

Love & Hip Hop: The Final Chapter airs on VH1, MTV and BET. It’s available to stream on Philo. Keep reading for more details on how cord-cutters can watch the limited reality TV series online with Philo.

A subscription to Philo Essential, which now comes with VH1, MTV and BET, gets you access to live TV from cable channels for just $25 per month — which is very affordable.

In fact, the live TV streaming platform features more than 70 cable networks, such as AMC, A&E, Animal Planet, BBC America, Bravo, Cartoon Network, CMT, Comedy Central, Discovery Channel, EarthX, Food Network, Game Show Network, Hallmark Channel, HGTV, History Channel, Investigation Discovery, IFC, Lifetime, Logo, MotorTrend, MotorTrends, Nickelodeon, OWN, Paramount Network, Pop TV, Revolt, Smithsonian Channel, Sundance TV, TLC, UPtv, Vice, We TV and many others. Plus, Philo has more than 170 FAST channels (Free Ad-Supported Streaming Television) available to watch too. Overall, that’s more than 240 channels, in total.

Starting on Tuesday (Nov. 10), you can watch Love & Hip Hop: The Final Chapter on VH1, as well as MTV and BET, with Philo. The six-part limited reality TV series is available to stream on Philo Essential with the service’s seven-day free trial.

Mississippi Senate: Scott Colom extends tight lead over Hyde-Smith as ballot deadlines hit

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Democratic nominee Scott Colom added slightly to his narrow lead over incumbent Sen. Cindy Hyde-Smith in the Mississippi Senate election as two key ballot deadlines hit Tuesday.

Colom, a local district attorney, as of 4:30 p.m. ET, was ahead of Hyde-Smith by 2,607 votes, out of 953,547 votes counted, with an estimated 97% of the votes in, according to the Associated Press. That's a margin of just 0.27 percentage points.

Independent candidate Ty Pinkins had 20,034 votes.

Tuesday is the deadline for resolving affidavit or provisional ballots, which are cast by voters whose eligibility is disputed or unclear on Election Day. It is also the deadline for the receipt of mail-in absentee ballots that are postmarked by Election Day, Nov. 3.

Mississippi is one of just two states whose Senate election has not been called. Alaska is the other.

Democrats so far have flipped seven Senate seats held by Republicans going into the election. That gives Democrats a new majority in the Senate of at least 54 seats in the 100-member chamber come January.

Alaska before Election Day was viewed as possibly key to the question of whether Republicans would retain their narrow majority in the Senate.

Mississippi, which has not elected a Democratic senator since the segregationist John Stennis in 1982, was on few political analysts' radar as a possible pickup for Democrats. If Colom wins his race, he would be the first Black senator from Mississippi since the post-Civil War Reconstruction Era.

In Alaska, Democratic nominee Mary Peltola is ahead by just 2,517 votes over incumbent Republican Sen. Dan Sullivan, with 218,641 total votes counted, a margin of 1.1 percentage points.

Peltola has 48.6% of the total vote. If neither she nor Sullivan gets more than 50% of the total vote, the race will go to a ranked-choice round, in which the two other candidates, Gerald Heikes and another candidate named Dan Sullivan, will be eliminated, and the second choices of voters who made them their first choice will be allocated between Peltola and the incumbent.

Remember When James Bond Was an Adidas Guy?

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

The search for the next James Bond continues. Over the weekend, Deadline reported that Robert Pattinson and Nicholas Hoult had also screen-tested for the role, in addition to Callum Turner, Jack Lowden, and Josh O'Connor. But once we've worked out who's getting the tux, there's another question: What sneakers is he going to wear?

As it turns out, their predecessor Daniel Craig got that bit right, too. In 2012's Skyfall, 007 turns up for his MI6 fitness tests in a navy SIS tracksuit and Adidas Gazelle IIs. In the scene, Bond's trying to prove he's fit enough to get back to work, and it's not going particularly well. As a viewer, you'd be forgiven for missing the shoes.

Daniel Craig as James Bond in Skyfall (2012).

But take another look. These were off-the-shelf Gazelles from Adidas's spring 2011 line, the sort that anyone could have picked up the year before the film came out. Bond is a guy whose wardrobe gets examined down to the last cufflink, and there he was in a regular pair of Adidas. His pair came in “dark indigo” suede with “Argentina blue” stripes, white rubber soles, and gold Gazelle lettering along the sides.

(There's a slight difference between the shoes a civilian could buy and the ones in the movie, though. The retail pair had a light blue heel tab, while Bond's appears darker. The fan website Bond Lifestyle suggests the costume department may have changed it, although that hasn't been officially confirmed.)

The Gazelle launched in 1966 as a multipurpose training shoe, and was the German sportswear giant's first-ever sneaker made from suede. The choice to put Bond in the shoe felt notable in more ways than one: In the training scene, the slim sole kept things nice and light underfoot, and the color combo went perfectly with his trackies. But there's also the Gazelle's place in British culture, from the football terraces in the ’80s to the Britpop crowd in the ’90s. So, it makes sense for a Gen-X British spy.

By 2021, Adidas had an official collab to go with No Time to Die. The collection featured all Ultraboost running shoes, with slight nods to Bond's tuxedos and Safin's porcelain mask. The “Q Branch” edition even had a hidden compartment in the lacing cage, handy if your gym shorts don't have pockets.

For a pair that came from a regular ol' Adidas collection, Bond's OG Gazelles are surprisingly difficult to find now. But if you fancy something similar without spending the next six months refreshing eBay, you can start with these.

A version of this story originally appeared in British GQ.

Democrats oppose plan to stack FCC with Republicans, plan probes of Chairman Carr

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Democrats accuse FCC nominee of evading questions about Carr, her current boss.

Senate Democrats today opposed President Trump’s nominee for an open Federal Communications Commission seat, and criticized Republicans for moving to confirm a new commissioner during the period before Democrats take control of Congress in January 2027. Democrats also plan to use their majority next year to launch probes of FCC Chairman Brendan Carr.

“After the American people last week overwhelmingly rejected the MAGA agenda, there is no justification for this committee to advance partisan nominees during the lame-duck period,” Sen. Ed Markey (D-Mass.) said today at the Commerce Committee’s nomination hearing for Danielle Thumann Severs, Trump’s pick to fill an empty commissioner’s seat.

Thumann Severs, an attorney and senior counsel to Carr, faced questions from Democrats about whether she supports Carr’s attacks on TV broadcasters.

“Do you support Chairman Carr’s illegal campaign to remove Jimmy Kimmel from the nation’s airwaves?” Sen. Ben Ray Luján (D-N.M.) asked the nominee.

Thumann Severs responded that she has not advised Carr on media-related topics and declined Luján’s request to identify any specific policy issues on which she disagrees with Carr.

Luján predicted trouble for Carr when Democrats take the majority in both chambers of Congress. He seemed to suggest that Carr will quickly grow tired of having to testify in Congress multiple times a month under the Democratic majorities.

“I don’t think Chairman Carr is going to be around more than another three or four months because when we have the gavels in here, he will be here once a month,” Luján said. “And Mr. Carr, if you’re listening now, know you’re going to be in this body once a month, if not more, and in the House of Representatives as well, answering questions, and you don’t have the protection of the gavel any longer.” Luján said Democrats will use “subpoena authorities that you have to respond to so we can get to the bottom of the nonsense and the cover-ups that have been taking place.”

Markey accused Thumann Severs of evading questions about Carr threatening and taking actions against broadcasters on behalf of Trump. “Anyone seeking a seat on the Federal Communications Commission must be willing to stand up to such threats and to defend the First Amendment, and you’re not willing to do so here in your confirmation hearing,” Markey said.

Democrats similarly criticized Thumann Severs for not giving a clear answer on whether the FCC has authority to eliminate E-Rate, a $2 billion-a-year program that was created by Congress to connect students and library visitors to the Internet. Carr has proposed eliminating or scaling back the program. Thumann Severs said she helped Carr work on the E-Rate Notice of Proposed Rulemaking, which seeks public comment about the program and the FCC’s authority to change or eliminate it.

“I can’t prejudge the open proceeding [on E-Rate] at the FCC right now,” she said today.

Sen. Tammy Duckworth (D-Ill.) criticized Thumann Severs’ answers. “If you’re confirmed, you’re going to be one of the votes that decides whether the FCC stays an independent regulator, or becomes an arm of the White House press office. We deserve to know where you stand, and I am not comfortable with your answers today,” Duckworth said.

Duckworth told Thumann Severs that the FCC cannot eliminate E-Rate without Congressional approval. “We never directed the FCC to eliminate the program and frankly, at a time when the price of gas, groceries, and utilities is skyrocketing, the last thing the FCC should be doing is making it harder for schools to stay connected,” Duckworth said.

The Senate Commerce, Science & Transportation Committee held today’s hearing for Thumann Severs and three other nominees for federal positions. Senators have until Thursday to submit additional questions for the record, and nominees will have until November 16 to respond.

Thumann Severs needs approval from the committee and then the full Senate to be confirmed to the FCC. Committee Chairman Ted Cruz (R-Texas) is trying to get her approved before Republicans have to give up their Senate majority.

Thumann Severs would give Republicans an unusual 3-1 advantage on the FCC, which usually operates with a 3-2 split. Democrats criticized Republicans for not pairing her nomination with a Democratic one. The commission has only one Democrat, Anna Gomez, and she would have to leave at the end of 2027 if she is not given another term.

“For many decades, the Senate has paired commission nominees: A Republican and a Democrat moving together,” Sen. John Hickenlooper (D-Colo.) said. “Congress established the FCC as an independent, bipartisan agency… a bipartisan FCC is essential to avoid things like large-scale media consolidation, and I think also more fundamentally to protect a free and diverse press.”

Because US law requires the FCC to have three commissioners for a quorum, Gomez’s “presence in the current 2-1 Republican majority is necessary for the FCC to conduct business,” Hickenlooper said. The Communications Act prohibits a single party from holding more FCC seats than is necessary to form a majority, but Hickenlooper predicted that Trump will fire Gomez to give Republicans a 3-0 stranglehold.

“If today’s nominee is confirmed, President Trump could then fire Commissioner Gomez and have an FCC with no Democratic voice at all,” Hickenlooper said.

Hickenlooper questioned whether Thumann Severs is sufficiently independent from Carr. “Ms. Thumann Severs, your career has largely been spent serving Brendan Carr,” Hickenlooper said. “[You are] currently Chairman Carr’s senior counsel; previously, you were legal advisor to Commissioner Carr. Your official duties include near-daily meetings with Chairman Carr and working to advance his agenda, amplify his messaging.”

Markey asked Thumann Severs if it was appropriate for Carr to use the FCC’s regulatory authority to pressure broadcasters into taking a comedian off the air. Markey was referring to an incident last year in which Carr threatened to revoke ABC broadcast TV licenses over remarks made by Kimmel, who was then suspended by ABC owner Disney.

“We can do this the easy way or the hard way,” Carr said at the time, adding that Disney must “take action” on Kimmel “or there’s going to be additional work for the FCC ahead.”

When asked about Carr’s comments today, Thumann Severs said, “Those were his words. They were not my words, and I have not advised him or worked with the chairman on these particular issues. I can say that if I were to be confirmed as commissioner, I would make all of my decisions [based on] the facts and the law and the record.”

Senate Democrats tried to get Thumann Severs to discuss Carr’s decision to order an early review of ABC station licenses. “Do you think the FCC should revoke broadcasters’ licenses because the president does not like their coverage?” Duckworth asked.

Thumann Severs answered that the FCC “has a responsibility to fully investigate any complaint… and to evaluate that complaint on the merits. I don’t think a decision should be made purely based on someone’s discontent with the content being aired.”

Although the ABC license review is widely seen as retaliation against the network for its news coverage and other programming, Carr’s proceeding is ostensibly based on an allegation that Disney’s diversity, equity, and inclusion (DEI) practices are discriminatory.

“Based on publicly available material, which is all I have seen on that particular matter, the licenses were called for early renewal as part of an investigation into potential rule violations,” Thumann Severs said today.

Cruz mentioned the Carr FCC’s review of ABC licenses and noted that “ABC argues [the review] is actually in retaliation for the network’s programming.” Cruz also alleged that in Democratic administrations, “the FCC has previously used its public interest standard as a weapon against conservatives.”

“If you are confirmed, will you commit to basing licensing decisions on the law and the record and to not using the FCC’s authority to pressure or punish a broadcaster for protected speech?” Cruz asked the nominee. She answered, “Yes.”

Cruz then asked Thumann Severs if she supports the JAWBONE Act, a bill that Cruz introduced with Sen. Ron Wyden (D-Ore.) to enable lawsuits against federal officials who try to coerce broadcasters or tech platforms into restricting speech.

Thumann Severs answered that she supports the bill. “I think it’s incredibly important that federal officials not use their position… to coerce against protected speech,” she said.

Cruz seemed satisfied with her answers. While Democrats recognized that Republicans have the votes to advance the nomination, they spent much of the hearing criticizing Thumann Severs’ answers and the Republican plan to confirm her without a paired Democratic nominee.

“We should not be confirming a Republican nominee without also renominating and confirming Anna Gomez, the Democrat, and filling the other vacant Democratic seat,” Markey said. “There is a potential next year where there will be three Republicans and no Democrats on the Federal Communications Commission… that is wrong, and that is why I do not believe we should proceed with this nomination at this time.”

The Subpoenas Are Coming, the Subpoenas Are Coming: Tech Execs Brace for Congressional Crackdown, Say Lobbyists

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

On Thursday, I was on the phone with a source when he suddenly sounded like he’d gotten the wind knocked out of him. We were in the middle of talking about antitrust policy when he exhaled and said, “Wow. Worst one-day destruction of value in history. Just got the Wall Street Journal alert.”

The tech world—and the markets built on top of it—is still figuring out how to feel about last week’s momentous Democratic midterm sweep. My Republican sources, unsurprisingly, reported that the vibes in their ranks are “horrendous.” Anyone who works with data centers is just about ready to throw in the towel on planet Earth and take their infrastructure efforts to space, with voters having opposed data center construction via 40 different ballot measures. And then there’s the more general rebuke of the future the tech industry is building, and the possible implications for an economy that’s increasingly come to revolve around it.

Indeed, it seems that the debate over AI has really, truly broken through to the mainstream this year: According to an NBC exit poll, 56% of voters called AI and data centers a “crucial or major factor” in their votes for Congress. Amid the free-for-all of President Trump’s first two years back in office, AI companies have been allowed to develop their technology largely free of regulatory oversight, but they may now be forced to pump the brakes.

In a lengthy interview with Ross Douthat on CBS’s 60 Minutes, Trump said he was open to working with Democrats to implement some AI guardrails. Still, no one I spoke to this week told me they expect to see sweeping AI legislation—unless, as two different tech Democrats suggested, there is an AI-caused catastrophe that “scares the shit out of us.”

Democratic Senators like Bernie Sanders and Elizabeth Warren have already begun making plans to investigate tech corporations.

In the absence of such an event, “we expect [new AI laws] to be narrow and bipartisan. Child safety, AI agent liability, and predeployment testing, for example, all have Republican cosponsors,” said Christina Ho, an executive at the financial auditing firm Oath Verified.

Meanwhile, even more explicitly MAGA-coded industries could see more concrete crackdowns. Those in “the crypto world,” who spent the first year of Trump 2.0 flying high, now “realize they’re screwed,” said an executive at a conservative think tank—especially as famed crypto critic Sherrod Brown makes his big return to the Senate with, as one Democratic strategist put it, an “axe to grind.”

Defense-tech founders and investors may also find themselves toning down their rhetoric as the testosterone-fueled “Department of War” era comes to an end with the possible impeachment of Secretary Pete Hegseth. In recent years, “dual use” has become a bit of a “dirty word,” said Cody Huggins, a partner at Scout Ventures. He suspects that promoting the commercial applications of military technologies, from satellites to drones, “will come back to be a little bit more sexy than it has been.”

Perhaps the biggest winners of the post-midterms period will be the DC law firms brought on to help manage the flood of subpoenas promised by politicians like Bernie Sanders and Elizabeth Warren. “AI executives might be called to testify,” said Ho, adding, “Companies would be wise to prepare their executives for the witness table before they prepare for any new laws.”

A Democratic tech lobbyist told me that one of his clients has already received a list of questions from a congressional committee. “They’re not waiting to have gavels,” he said.

Luther Lowe, the head of public policy at the start-up accelerator Y Combinator, is hoping to see a return to the antitrust revolution once promised by former FTC chair Lina Khan. “I think some of that stuff could come back online,” he said, referring to antitrust legislation that then Senate majority leader Chuck Schumer declined to bring to a vote in 2022.

In the future, tech executives who went all in on MAGA (right after going all in on woke) may want to consider some age-old advice: just be yourself. “Companies need to stop thinking about political outcomes as permanent conditions. Regulations change, governments change, and priorities change. That’s not new,” said Andrew Peek, CEO of the investing platform Tilt. “What’s different now is the sheer amount of money being committed to technologies whose economics are still evolving. If your entire strategy only works under one political scenario, that’s a pretty big risk to be taking.”

Still, there was one midterm fight in which the tech world definitively won out: the one over Proposition 40, or California’s so-called billionaire tax, which was defeated. Some suspect that the hundreds of millions of dollars spent by Silicon Valley billionaires to defeat the measure could backfire. “There’s a growing wave of anger and resentment from the working class that is coming for them,” said the Democratic strategist. The irony of spending so much money to fight the tax, he said, is that if it had passed, it could have “temporarily removed some of the pressure in the pressure cooker.” All that money having been spent to shut down the tax, he warned, “is gonna make things much worse.”

Amid Bethesda takeover and Fallout pivot, Obsidian's finally making good on The Outer Worlds 2 DLC

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Part one of Side Effects Guaranteed launches in December

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The Outer Worlds 2 is finally getting its DLC. Obsidian has announced that Side Effects Guaranteed, the first of a "two-part expansion" series, will launch on December 2. The devs had been promising DLC since before launch as part of the RPG's $100 Premium Edition, but you'd be forgiven for worrying that those promises were forgotten amid the massive shakeup at the studio.

The first part of Side Effects Guaranteed will offer a new region to explore on Eden, a boosted level cap with new perks grab, four new flaws to spice up your build, new gear, and an additional radio station. The story will have you working with one of two Sub Rosa agents as you decide what to do with the hibernating crew of a derelict ship.

We spoke with Obsidian about The Outer Worlds 2 DLC shortly after the game's launch last year, and at that time they'd only begun to sketch out plans for the expansion. But at least one thing has carried over from those high-level ideas: "Maybe we'll lean a bit harder into some of the more minor factions, like Sub Rosa, and build them out a little bit," game director Brandon Adler told us in 2025, and that part of the plan seems to have borne out.

But a lot has happened at Obsidian since we had that chat. It's now under Bethesda's direct management and working on a new Fallout game as part of Xbox's big shakeup. Obsidian was not immune to the mass layoffs at the publisher, and seemingly had an Avowed sequel stripped away as Xbox pointed it to return to the more lucrative Fallout franchise.

While The Outer Worlds 2 was one of our favorite games of 2025, it never appeared to make much of a splash among the broader gaming community. Its fans feared that slow sales might lead to the DLC being canceled, and there's still some fear that the expansions have been truncated so Obsidian can move onto Fallout more quickly.

Monza, Baldissoni: “L’obiettivo è tenere con Varela. Napoli? Tutto prematuro”

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Mauro Baldissoni, amministratore delegato del Monza, ha rilasciato alcune dichiarazioni su Gustavo Varela ai microfoni di Sky Sport: “È troppo facile menzionare un solo giocatore e prendersi i meriti adesso, siamo stati bravi a identificarlo, ma è stato bravo lui a dimostrare il suo valore. Sappiamo che ogni volta che si fa uno step c’è bisogno di tempo per adattarsi, c’è da fare uno sforzo. Lui lo ha fatto egregiamente, speriamo che non si fermi e che continui, ha ricevuto tantissime attenzioni, suggerirei di togliergliele… Lui ora è sereno, tranquillo, sa che non ha fatto nulla, ma ha mostrato presupposti che fanno presagire a una grande carriera. Non so quanto riusciremo ad avere con noi. Resterà al Napoli? della competitività della Serie A, moltissimi sono alla prima esperienza, tutti stanno dimostrando potenzialità enormi, speriamo le facciano vedere in fretta, abbiamo bisogno di punti (ride, ndr). Venderlo a qualcuno tenendolo fino a giugno può essere un’idea? Nonostante si debba lavorare per tempo, siamo a metà novembre, non abbiamo questi discorsi con nessuno, leggiamo che c’è interesse, alcune sollecitazioni le ha avute il giocatore tramite il suo agente e qualcosa che abbiamo ricevuto anche noi, ma siamo molto lontani dal parlare di trattative, è tutto molto prematuro. Che atmosfera si è respirata oggi al Social Football Summit in un momento in cui si parla anche di ricorsi in merito alla futura elezione del presidente della FIGC? L’avrei evitata volentieri questa domanda, devo essere accorto su come rispondere. È un argomento che meriterebbe molta considerazione, invece è la seconda volta che non se ne parla nemmeno in una riunione della Lega Serie A. Se finge sia un tema che non interessa, mi dispiace la deriva che stiamo prendendo. Usciamo dopo un’elezione Federale con risultati evidenti, ha vinto un candidato e sostenuto dalla Lega Serie A, è stato ribadito anche in un comunicato e adesso non tutti ritengono che sia ancora così. Al di là di qualche adesione, non se ne parla. Dover finire a discutere di ricorsi in tribunale ancora prima dell’elezione è una scelta sbagliata, ci sarebbe l’obbligo di confrontarsi, la Serie A rivendica di essere il motore del sistema… Mi sarebbe piaciuto discuterne”.

Bella Hadid Purchases $5.5 Million ‘Enchanting Creekside Compound’ in Colorado

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Supermodel Bella Hadid is Colorado-bound.

The Victoria’s Secret angel snapped up a gorgeous creekside compound in Crested Butte, CO, according to the Wall Street Journal.

Hadid, 30, purchased the three-bedroom, three-bathroom retreat for $5.5 million—a sharp decrease from its $6,995,000 asking price, set in June this year.

Listing agent Channing Boucher of LIV Sotheby’s International Realty told the publication that the daughter of the former “Real Housewives of Beverly Hills” star has already resided elsewhere in the Crested Butte area for “several months.”

In fact, she recently gushed about the state in a Nov. 9 Instagram post, shared after attending a Colorado Avalanche hockey game. Hadid posted that she's a "Colorado girl at heart" and that it "was only a matter of time before" she would find her way to the team.

It seems as if Hadid has found a perfect slice of the Centennial State to call her own.

“Tucked away on a private alley in the most desired west side of town, this enchanting creekside compound is a secret sanctuary, impossible to replicate in character or setting,” the listing boasted.

The home is nestled on a 7,841-square-foot lot with 200 feet of Coal Creek frontage. While the dwelling itself was constructed in 2001, it’s a historical property that incorporates two “original 100-year-old coal sheds,” which have been lovingly restored to serve as livable spaces.

Inside, an open floor plan features “soaring 30-foot ceilings,” and design details salvaged from century-old structures include the oversized ceilings beams sourced from a historic Columbia River warehouse, along with iron bolts and other charming items.

“The line between old and new vanish in the craftsmanship,” reads the listing, which also touts the weathered lumber used to construct the exterior and the 18th-century Virgina barn hardwood floors.

It’s easy to see why Hadid would choose the idyllic property as an escape. The listing explains that the home is in a secluded spot at the end of a dead-end gravel lane with only one other home, “free from traffic, tourists, and noise.”

The primary suite is in its own wing and constructed from a former coal shed. Skylights provide stunning views of the natural landscape, while the luxurious bathroom makes for a comfortable sanctuary.

The outdoor space is a striking complement to the intentionally designed home. It includes a creekside patio for dining and entertaining, and a soothing hot tub is located just at the edge of the water.

Though the home seems as if it exists in a world of its own, it’s not far from the vibrant attractions Crested Butte has to offer, including upscale dining and boutique shopping.

Hadid's family has been known to seek out residences that allow them to connect with nature. Mom Yolanda Hadid has a stunning Pennsylvania farm, which she previously told Realtor.com® is a place where she can “heal and reconnect with Mother Earth.”

"Eight years ago, after my divorce and years of battling chronic neurological Lyme disease, I needed to get away from the noise of city life," she explained. "I longed for a sanctuary where I could heal and reconnect with Mother Earth just the way I grew up in Holland.

"When I found the farm, I instantly fell in love. … There were no gates at that time, so I parked on the main road and walked down the long driveway; the energy was unbelievable. I instantly knew I was home even before I saw the buildings."

Yolanda listed the property for $10.88 million at the end of 2025, but pulled it off the market just three months later. However, it is understood that the delisting is temporary, and the property will most likely return to the market in the future.

On this day in space! Nov. 10, 2013: Europe's GOCE satellite, the 'Ferrari of space,' fell out of orbit after mapping Earth's gravity field

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Controllers weren't sure where GOCE would fall back to Earth. Some people worried that it might crash into a populated area.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

On Nov. 10, 2013, Europe's GOCE satellite fell to Earth after four years in orbit mapping the planet's gravitational field.

GOCE, its name was short for Gravity field and steady-state Ocean Circulation Explorer, launched in 2009 on a mission to map Earth's gravity like never before. But despite that successful mission, it was doomed in 2013 when it ran out of fuel.

At the time, controllers weren't sure where GOCE would fall back to Earth. Some people worried that it might crash into a populated area. However, the small spacecraft broke up in the atmosphere and didn't cause any property damage.

It disintegrated about 50 miles above the Falkland Islands in the South Atlantic Ocean. The European Space Agency called the mission a success. GOCE lasted far longer than expected. It showed small variations in Earth's gravity, mapped the structure of Earth's crust and mantle, and tracked ocean currents.

The GOCE satellite was a sleek spacecraft, with fins covered in solar panels that gave it a futuristic design. In fact, its look earned it a pretty cool nickname.

"GOCE's sleek, elegant aerodynamic design — responsible for it being dubbed the 'Ferrari of space' — immediately set it apart from most other satellites,." ESA officials said in a description.

There was more than looks behind GOCE's design. In order to generate the best map ever of Earth's gravitational field, GOCE had to fly in an orbit so low — just 139 miles (224 kilometers) — it needed to counteract the drag from the gossamer thin atmosphere of Earth at that altitude. The International Space Station orbits Earth at nearly twice that altitude.

GOCE was powered by an xenon ion engine that allowed to maintain its low orbit for four years. Once it ran out of fuel, the 1-ton satellite could not maintain its position in space, leading to a fiery plunge to Earth.

The large size of GOCE — it weighed 2,425 pounds (1,100 kilograms) and was 17 feet (5.3 meters) long and 3.2 feet (1 m) wide — made its reentry stand out, particularly since at least 25% of the satellite was expected to reach the Earth's surface.

Forward secured a $50 million credit line and committed up to $50 million to a tokenized U.S. home-price product.

Forward Industries added roughly 226,000 SOL to its treasury through purchases and staking rewards since the end of September.

The company said Tuesday that its holdings reached approximately 8.7 million SOL, worth roughly $726.5 million, totaling about 1.5% of Solana's total circulating supply.
